A thousand cavalrymen and their mounts, idle for two days behind stone walls, turned restless. The stables grew foul, and the men sat along the barrack verandahs in the cold air, trading rumours about the figure seen on the river terrace.
The cleansing of the sanctuary had taken the whole day, scrubbing the marble plinth and the altar stone with vinegar, lustral water, and sand to scour away the dried blood of the vow.
The following day, a courier had arrived from Moguntiacum carrying the first official dispatch confirming what Raeticus had feared. Lorch had suffered an attack and the garrison had retreated to the Rhine. Raeticus had then ordered the expiatory sacrifice be made.
A black ram had been led up the principia steps, and the formal piaculum offered to Mars Ultor to lift the curse from the command. But the reading of the entrails had settled the matter. When the belly was opened, the principal lobe of the liver—the caput—was missing entirely, withered to a knot of gristle. The haruspex had dropped his bronze knife, backed out of the alcove on his knees, and refused to speak the ritual blessing.
The god had not accepted the offering. The curse remained, and Raeticus knew he was doomed.
By evening, Raeticus was in the room off the basilica. The lamp smoked on the table, casting shadows across the unread rosters. His left arm hung stiffly beneath his cloak, wrapped in thick linen, steeped in the yellow fluid weeping from the wound, the fever burning behind his eyes.
A cavalry horn sounded from the south gate — the three-note call of an imperial salute.
Marcius turned towards the yard as iron-shod hooves clattered across the gravel apron, moving at the steady, hard trot of fresh mounts. Voices called out at the barrier, and boots rang on the flagstones of the great hall.
Tribune Valerius came into the room. His face was lined with the fatigue of twenty-eight hours in the saddle. Behind him on the steps, nine legionary troopers held the reins of their remounts.
Valerius halted three paces from the table and brought his fist to his armour.
"Praefectus Raeticus."
Raeticus got to his feet, using the timber edge to steady himself. He pulled his cloak around his left side to conceal the bandaged hand.
"Tribune Valerius," Raeticus said, his voice hoarse. "You've ridden from Castra Regina."
"Dispatched yesterday at noon," Valerius said. His eyes took in the room, the smell of vinegar, and the fever-sweat on the prefect's forehead. "Legatus Flaccus received your dispatch. Two draught horses at your gate, smoke to the north, and the Ala marching for Dalkingen."
Valerius looked past him, through the window, towards the blackened wreckage of the south horreum.
"The Legatus sent me to find the Ala," Valerius said. "If you were engaged and needed infantry support, I was ordered to send a rider back to him at once. He has eight hundred men of the First and Second Cohorts ready to move the moment I send word."
Valerius kept his voice level. "What is the situation here, Praefectus?"
Raeticus swallowed, his throat dry.
"We reached Dalkingen," he said. "The post was destroyed. Eight auxiliaries dead. While we were attempting to recover the bronze from the archway, the vault gave way. Heat-stress. The stone collapsed into the passage and buried the statue."
"And the force that burnt the post?"
Raeticus paused before answering. "We found no tracks."
"And your storehouse?"
"A night raid," Raeticus said, his uninjured hand tightening on the edge of the table. "While the Ala was returning. Four dead at the postern. They took medical stores, iron points, and provisions. The fire took the timber roof."
Valerius listened in silence, his gaze resting on the stained linen wrapping showing beneath Raeticus's cloak.
"And since then?" Valerius asked. "Have you pursued?"
"The Ala has been held in quarters," Raeticus said.
Valerius looked at him for a long moment. "A thousand horse held inside the walls for two days, and no patrols on the road."
Raeticus said nothing.
"Legatus Flaccus is holding two cohorts under arms at Castra Regina waiting for my rider," Valerius said. "I need to know what word to send him, Praefectus. Are you in contact with an enemy, and do you require the infantry?"
Raeticus stood stiffly in the lamplight, the fever throbbing through his arm, the memory of the empty platter in the shrine still hanging over him.
"There is no enemy in sight, Tribune," Raeticus said quietly. "The frontier stone stands. The road is open. Tell the Legatus there is nothing here for his cohorts to fight."

23 — Acting Command
Tribune Valerius looked at the man standing before him in the lamplight. Raeticus was shaking with fever, clutching a rotting wrist beneath his cloak, and claiming the frontier stood secure.
A legionary staff officer riding twenty-eight hours under the direct seal of Legio III Italica dealt in visible facts. Within an hour of his arrival, Valerius had the camp medicus brought to the principia to shear away the soiled linen. The cut showed black and putrid to the bone, and Raeticus's speech had already dissolved into delirium. By sunrise, Valerius had relieved the Praefectus Alae of duty and transferred him under armed guard to a cot in the fort's valetudinarium.
Command of an auxiliary cavalry regiment lay outside a legionary tribune's authority. Valerius held the governor's mandate by extension, empowered to establish order and name a temporary commander until the governor appointed a new Praefectus Alae. He assembled the decurions in the principia and placed Decurion Senecio in charge of the garrison. Senecio had shown steady judgement on the road from Lorch and possessed the spine to speak the truth.
Three of Valerius's troopers rode south along the paved highway to Castra Regina at first light. The dispatch to Legatus Flaccus laid out the facts - Praefectus Raeticus lay incapacitated by blood-poison; Decurion Senecio stood in as temporary commander of the Ala; Valerius remained at Aalen to oversee the inquiry and await the governor's appointments or the arrival of the legionary cohorts.
Valerius and Senecio turned their combined authority towards the unraveling of the garrison. They summoned Decurion Marcius to the record room to lay bare the mechanics of every engagement, paring away rumours of omens and forest spectres.
Senecio reviewed the western road. He described an enemy who paced an armed cavalry column from absolute concealment, planting heavy shafts into harness buckles, saddle horns, and water skins with cold, exacting precision. They had spared the troopers' lives purely to seed panic, leaving an intact, abandoned fort behind them and a hearth built for a mere handful of riders.
Marcius followed with the disaster at Dalkingen. He detailed an entire cavalry wing lured twelve miles up the valley to find the gate collapsed into a mountain of smoking rubble, an imperial shrine felled by the post's own draught beasts, and an exhausted Ala returning to find its home ramparts trembling at two arrows dropped into an empty courtyard.
Valerius listened in silence, rolling a recovered broadhead across his knuckles. To a trained legionary staff officer, the pattern stood out in sharp relief. A handful of lethal, highly disciplined adversaries had dissected the command from within. They had measured Raeticus's pride, manipulated his obsessions, and tricked the garrison into defeating itself.
Senecio and Valerius halted every pursuit into the surrounding timber, directing all available manpower towards the interior of the base. They suppressed rumours of curses and underworld reapers with the vine-staff, punishing superstition with docked pay and hard bread rations. To protect the thousand mounts from lameness and colic, Senecio broke the stall confinement through structured, heavily guarded rotations. Squadrons moved under archer overwatch to muck stalls, air bedding, and walk the horses down to the gravel shallows of the Kocher for water. Work crews cleared the charred timbers of the south horreum, blacksmiths refired the armoury forges to repair damaged tack, and sentry rosters on the ramparts doubled.
Over the next few days, Aalen hardened into a disciplined redoubt. Routine and physical labour drove out fear.
Five days after Valerius reached Aalen, the courier from Castra Regina arrived with orders from the Legatus.
Valerius received the dispatch in the record room. Flaccus approved the removal of Praefectus Raeticus to the valetudinarium, and confirmed Decurion Senecio in temporary command of the Ala. Valerius was instructed to remain at Aalen to assist Senecio until the governor appointed a new Praefectus Alae. At the foot of the sheet, Flaccus added a clear command - send regular accounts of the true situation along the limes. The two cohorts of the Third Legion remained in their quarters at the Danube, awaiting word of the enemy before marching west.
The return courier prepared to ride south that same afternoon, bearing Valerius's official accounting of the disaster at Dalkingen.
Valerius had refused to set the shameful truth to wax. To report that two runaway draught horses had dragged the bronze emperor into a bonfire, and auxiliary pry-bars had collapsed the vault into rubble, invited disgrace upon the entire provincial command. Instead, Valerius invented a conventional engagement. An Alemannic war-band had crossed the palisade at dawn, set fire to the timber guardhouse, and battered the monument from its plinth. The infantry of Cohors III Thracum from Buch had advanced from their fort, driving the raiders back into the northern forests before the Ala arrived on the road. The cavalry had secured the valley, arriving, unfortunately, too late to salvage the imperial bronze. The dispatch assured Flaccus that the enemy stood repelled, the border remained quiet, and the Ala held the sector in hand.

24 — Rome
Vettius worked through the morning intake in the Tabularium. With early winter rains keeping the Tiber high, grain barges from Ostia were held up downriver, and Magistrate Caecilius was already at his elbow demanding harbor manifests before the urban prefect called for them.
The courier from Moguntiacum arrived before the fourth hour, his leather dispatch satchel water-stained from the road. The packet from Germania Superior carried the green wax of the provincial governor.
Vettius cracked the seal and brought the tallow lamp closer to read the hurried scrawl.
The forwarded notice was brief. Fort Lorch, the stone auxiliary base anchoring the limes bend where Germania Superior met Raetia, had suffered an attack. Fires had consumed the stables, granary, and stores beyond control. The Praefectus had ordered an immediate retreat toward Moguntiacum, abandoning the stone fort with the surviving cohort. The text omitted the numbers, tactics, and identity of the assailants.
Vettius passed the sheet across to Caecilius.
"Lorch has fallen," Vettius said. "The stone garrison at the bend."
Caecilius held it near the lamp, squinting through the smudged lines to the signature block.
"Lorch holds five hundred men behind stone ramparts and ditch lines," Caecilius said.
"The Praefectus evacuated the post," Vettius said. "He reports internal fires destroyed his quarters."
Caecilius dropped the sheet back onto the table.
"He abandoned permanent stone defenses because timber sheds caught fire," Caecilius said. "A brazier overturns in the straw, the stables burn, and the commander panics in the smoke. He flees thirty leagues to the Rhine, and leaves the enemy unnamed to excuse his cowardice."
"The governor forwarded it as an urgent loss," Vettius said.
"The governor wants coin to rebuild his stables," Caecilius replied. "I have sixty grain barges tied to the bank downstream, and the crowd at the Porticus Minucia will start throwing cobbles by sunset if the bread ovens go cold. If a real host had broken the frontier wall, the beacon towers along the Rems would have alarmed Raetia, and the cavalry at Aalen would have ridden. This is an auxiliary officer fleeing his own kitchen fire."
"Prepare the digest for Syria?" Vettius asked.
"Follow standard protocol," Caecilius said. "Draft the summary transmittal for the imperial court at Antioch. Note it as an internal post fire under investigation by the governor, and route the copy to the eastern post."

25 — Clearinghouse of Misery
The quiet at Aalen lasted a single day before reports began arriving, turning the record room into a clearinghouse of misery.
The day after the courier departed, a rider galloped in from Fort Böbingen. A timber wagon hauling seasoned oak from the Rems valley had been ambushed in a narrow defile four miles west of the fort. An arrow from the scrub had killed the lead escort instantly. Before the other two horsemen could clear their scabbards, attackers swarmed the road from both flanks. The auxiliary escort lay dead in the ditch, their chests split by heavy spears, the teamster dead across his seat. The attackers had sliced the traces, leaving the oxen wandering and the wagon standing abandoned in the road surrounded by corpses.
The following morning brought word of a strike along the northern wall. The watchtower near the collapsed Dalkingen arch had gone dark at dusk. A relief party from Fort Buch, twelve miles up the valley road, found both sentries dead on the catwalk, each pierced through the throat by a heavy trilobate arrow loosed from the timber three hundred paces off. The iron fire-basket lay quenched in the ditch.
At sunset the next day, simultaneous alarms struck opposite flanks of the district. Both garrisons kept their gates barred through the dark, their couriers galloping out at first light to reach Aalen before mid-morning.
From Fort Buch came word of a grain wagon hit outside the fort's own gates - the teamster killed, five sacks of barley slit open across the road, the draught mules cut loose into the scrub.
From Fort Böbingen arrived an account of pure butchery. A mounted patrol of four auxiliaries had been ambushed in the rocky defile three miles west of the fort. The strike had bypassed the men to slaughter the mounts. Low blades swept through the scrub, houghing every horse at the hock. All four beasts crashed screaming to the gravel, their hamstrings severed clean. Two riders died crushed beneath their mounts; a third was run through with a spear as he struggled to rise. The surviving auxiliary was stripped of armour and spatha, left standing in his under-tunic, and ordered to walk back to Böbingen on foot with a message - Walk. Keep walking. Tell your decurion Rome has lost its legs.
The next day brought word from the high western ridge. A dispatch courier riding from Fort Schirenhof towards Aalen had been killed on the road. The rider lay dead in the scrub with a fractured skull, his leather case slit and cohort manifests scattered into the dirt, his horse houghed at the tendon and left thrashing in a ditch.

26 — Moguntiacum
The sentry on the south gate tower of Moguntiacum leaned over the stone parapet, watching the road through the river fog. Along the southern approach, figures moved through the grey slush.
The sentry called down to Tribune Cassius on the walkway below.
"Sir! Movement on the south road. A crowd on foot with horses and carts."
Cassius climbed the rampart stair to the railing.
"Carters?" the sentry asked. "They move in a cluster. Some carry shields."
Cassius looked through the mist at the lead files, his eyes fixing on the tarnished brass of auxiliary helmets.
"That is Lorch," Cassius said.
Cassius leaned over the timber rail, calling down to the guard detail in the passage.
"Clear the barrier. Open the main portal."
Cassius descended the stone stairs as the column reached the outer ditch. The report received ten days earlier had announced the retreat, but the reality shocked the gate guards into silence. Auxiliary infantry filed past the barrier, their sandals worn to the raw leather and their legs caked in freezing clay. Mounted troopers rode alongside the foot soldiers, their cloaks stained with soot.
Centurion Rufinus marched at the head of the file. Cassius halted him on the gravel.
"Centurion. Report," Cassius ordered.
"Lorch garrison, Tribune," Rufinus answered, his breath steaming in the damp air. "Centurion Rufinus. Three hundred effective men remaining."
Behind the soldiers creaked seven farm carts, their axles groaning in the slush. Cassius stepped to the nearest cart, where dark fluid dripped through the floorboards.
"What is this cargo?" Cassius asked.
"Eighty casualties, sir," Rufinus said. "Men burned in the stables, and men struck by arrows during the night assault. The frost on the road turned the wounds foul."
The stench of gangrene drifted from the straw bedding. Cassius walked to the third cart. Centurion Marcellus sat propped against the timber rail, wrapped in coarse horse blankets, his jaw slack and his stare fixed on the axle hub.
"Marcellus," Cassius said.
The centurion kept his eyes on the iron hub, his mouth shut tight.
"He maintains that silence since his recovery in the woods," Rufinus said. "He remains mute."
Behind the wagons rode the Praefectus on a gaunt horse, his head hanging over the saddle horn, his face grey beneath dried road silt. Beside the mount walked Beneficiarius Ligarius, his leather document case strapped across his ribs.
"Ligarius," Cassius said, stepping before the horse.
Ligarius halted on the gravel.
"Tribune," Ligarius said.
"Bring the Praefectus," Cassius ordered. "The headquarters requires an immediate accounting."
Cassius led both men through the fortress to the record hall of Legio XXII Primigenia. Cornicularius Priscus sat at the cedar table with three staff centurions standing along the stone wall. Across the desk lay the dispatches from the previous weeks.
The Praefectus sank onto an oak stool, his hands trembling against his thighs.
Cassius tapped the upper documents with his finger.
"Your first dispatch arrived twelve days ago," Cassius said, looking at Ligarius. "It reported your investigation well in hand. A contained situation. Then, two days later, a second courier arrives announcing the fort is burning and the garrison is in full retreat. Explain this disparity."
Ligarius stood straight, clay flaking from his boots onto the flagstones.
"The assault fell after the first courier departed," Ligarius said, his voice hard. "The enemy held their main strength in reserve until our patrol was committed to the trees. The Alemanni crossed the limes in massive numbers. Thousands of warriors broke through the forest at dusk, surrounded the stone ramparts, and discharged volleys of fire arrows into the interior buildings."
"Did you identify their tribal banners?" Cassius asked.
"The assault hit four sectors simultaneously," Ligarius replied. "They deployed heavy archers in the tree line, targeted the mounts to restrict our cavalry, and forced the western approach. When the timber stores burned beyond control and the ramparts fell into confusion, the position collapsed. The Praefectus ordered the retreat to preserve the remaining force."
Cassius studied Ligarius, then looked down at the Praefectus, who sat nodding in dull agreement.
"Thousands," Cassius repeated.
"A general tribal uprising," Ligarius said. "The opening blow of a coordinated invasion."
Cassius turned to Priscus at the table.
"Enter the testimony into the provincial record," Cassius ordered. "Classify Lorch as destroyed by an overwhelming Alemannic force. Draft a dispatch for the governor and the legate. Tell them the frontier wall at the bend has fallen, and the barbarian tribes are massing in the forest."
"And Rome?" Priscus asked, his reed poised over the ledger.
"A priority dispatch for the capital," Cassius said. "The previous courier carried only the bare notice of the retreat. Draft the full operational accounting for the Senate and the chancellery. State that the limes at the bend has buckled under an invading horde, the auxiliary garrison has fallen back to the Rhine, and the province requires legionary reinforcement."

27 — Minor Disturbances
At Aalen, Valerius had no relief. By the close of the week, the pattern of the strikes reversed. Midday brought couriers from opposite flanks within an hour of each other.
From Fort Schirenhof, high on the western ridge, came word of the northern long-range archery and silent stealth. An auxiliary on the rampart had been dropped across the gorge by a heavy trilobate arrow through the shoulder. Simultaneously, in the morning mist, the fort's postern gate stood ajar, the guard dead against the stone from a lead-weighted Roman plumbata driven through his throat.
At that exact morning hour, the western horse-butchery struck the northern plain twelve miles away outside Buch. At a narrow culvert where the road dipped through marshy scrub, a mounted patrol of six horsemen riding towards Dalkingen had slowed to a walk. Figures burst from the roadside hazel. Low curved blades and heavy axes struck at the legs, houghing four cavalry horses in the road. An attacker dropped from an overhanging branch with a piercing cry, twin daggers flashing, gutting two riders before their boots touched the dirt. The surviving auxiliaries fled back to Buch on foot, their mounts dying behind them.
The attacks continued day after day. A supply cart overturned in a creek, its escort slaughtered in the shallows. Two remounts found dead in an outer paddock with severed hocks. An auxiliary scout picked off by an archer shooting from concealment while scouting a ford. Every morning brought fresh wax tablets to Aalen, each bearing news of dead auxiliaries, butchered mounts, and terrorised commanders.
Valerius dispatched riders back to Buch and Böbingen with an urgent order - interrogate the survivors and obtain exact descriptions of the assailants—their numbers, armour, and tribal origins.
The answers arrived by dusk.
From Böbingen, the commander forwarded the testimony of the stripped auxiliary. The soldier swore his patrol had fallen to women—fighters with braided hair, bronze armour, and curved blades.
From Buch, the report grew wilder still. The horsemen who fled the culvert insisted the screaming attacker in the tree canopy was a red-haired woman bearing blue paint across her cheekbones.
Valerius tossed the tablets onto the cedar table with an exasperated laugh.
"Women," Valerius said, his voice sharp with disgust. "Our cavalry breaks in panic, and the survivors invent fairy tales to excuse their shame."
"The guards at our own south postern swore to a woman holding a curved blade," Senecio observed.
"Raeticus indulged that superstition, and fever consumed his command," Valerius replied. "A Roman officer records military reality, Senecio. We face disciplined deserters who disguise their tracks, while our auxiliaries fabricate fables to mask their disgrace."
"Deserters or fables, they are bleeding us daily," Senecio said, his voice flat with anger. "They butcher our horses at the hock. They shoot sentries from three hundred paces, drop out of trees, and slip postern gates with Roman darts. Buch has barred its gates at noon. Schirenhof's horsemen refuse to mount patrols. Our outposts are crippled, stranded on foot, while the Ala sits behind stone."
"We preserve the Ala," Valerius insisted, his hand flattening against the timber. "If we send squadrons into those gorges, our mounts will be houghed in the defiles just like the rest."
"Then Aalen is an island," Senecio said. "A cavalry fort that leaves its satellite posts to be butchered on foot surrenders the border."
Senecio turned and walked out, his boots striking the flagstones.
Valerius sat alone in the lamplight. Two weeks had passed since his arrival, and the courier for Castra Regina was to ride at dawn. Flaccus awaited his accounting.
Valerius drew a fresh diptych across the table and smoothed the black wax with his stylus.
A Roman military dispatch required order - names of the fallen, tribes engaged, breaches in the line, banners taken. Here, the facts formed an impossible tally. Daily butcheries along the roads. Scores of auxiliaries dead. Cavalry mounts houghed at the tendon. Gatekeepers killed by Roman darts. Sentries dropped on catwalks. Messengers sent walking in their tunics. Tactical signatures trading ground across twenty miles of wilderness.
The sole description provided by the survivors named a band of women.
To set that claim to wax invited instant mockery from the Legatus. Flaccus would judge Valerius deranged, infected by provincial fever. To invent an invading army contradicted his own report from five days prior, when he had assured the Legatus that the Alemanni were driven off and the border stood secure.
Yet to report the true collapse of the frontier invited ruin upon his own career. A staff officer who confessed that twenty-four turmae of heavy horse sat paralysed inside stone while auxiliary outposts bled daily would face immediate dismissal.
Valerius lowered the point of the iron stylus. He pressed the metal into the black wax and carved a second lie.
He set down words of measured calm. The limes stood firm. The wall remained intact. The casualties represented isolated friction among the native settlements—occasional local disputes causing minor disturbances along the roads. The Ala held the district in hand, and the two cohorts of the Third Legion could remain at their posts on the Danube.
The courier had departed for Castra Regina barely an hour before another dispatch arrived from Moguntiacum detailing the scale of the attack that had forced the garrison to retreat from Lorch.

28 — The Warning
In the Tabularium at Rome, ten days after the first Lorch courier arrived, the post from Raetia reached the intake desk. Vettius took the sealed cylinder from the rider, checking the imprint in the red wax. It bore the eagle and the capricorn of Legio III Italica from Castra Regina.
Vettius cracked the wax and spread the papyrus across his desk. The dispatch carried the signature of Legatus Quintus Caecilius Flaccus.
Vettius read the text, his finger tracing the lines down the sheet. An Alemannic war-band had forced the palisade at dawn near Dalkingen. The raiders had torched the timber guardhouse, killed the eight auxiliaries on detachment, and battered the bronze monument of Divus Antoninus from its stone plinth. The infantry of Cohors III Thracum had advanced from Fort Buch, driving the host back into the northern forests. Praefectus Raeticus had deployed the entire cavalry force of Ala II Flavia from Aalen, marching a thousand heavy horse to secure the valley road, arriving after the bronze had suffered fatal damage.
Vettius stood and carried the sheet to Caecilius's table. Caecilius looked up from a requisition for grain storage.
"From Raetia," Vettius said, laying the sheet before him. "Castra Regina forwards a battle report from the frontier."
Caecilius read the dispatch, his mouth tightening. He leaned over the table, his gaze locked on the lines detailing the cavalry deployment.
"The entire Ala," Caecilius said. "A thousand horse called into the field."
"And the monument at Dalkingen destroyed," Vettius added. "The bronze emperor toppled."
Caecilius pushed his grain requisition aside and drew the frontier ledger closer.
"Bring the map of the northern limes," Caecilius said.
Vettius brought the rolled sheepskin from the archive rack and spread it across the desk, anchoring the corners with bronze weights.
Caecilius traced the boundary line with an iron stylus. His stylus halted at the sharp angle where the split-oak palisade of Germania Superior met the stone wall of Raetia.
"Ten days ago, we received the report that Lorch had fallen," Caecilius said, tapping the ink mark on the Germanic side of the bend. "Thirty miles to the east sits Dalkingen, on the Raetian side. That is two provincial sectors struck within a few weeks of each other."
"Lorch was reported as an internal fire," Vettius said.
"That was our assumption," Caecilius said, his voice hard. "Now Raetia reports an assault on an imperial shrine, eight dead soldiers, and a general muster of the largest cavalry unit on the Danube border. Barbarians are crossing the boundary at will. They strike Lorch, and then they strike Dalkingen."
Caecilius looked up from the sheepskin, his eyes sharp.
"This is an organized offensive across two commands," Caecilius said. "The frontier wall is breached."
"Legatus Flaccus reports the raiders repelled," Vettius observed. "He states the border stands secure."
"He states his cavalry arrived after the gate was destroyed," Caecilius countered. "When an auxiliary commander sends a thousand horse to chase raiders into the brush, he is frightened. The tribes know the bend of the limes is weak."
Caecilius stood and paced the flagstones behind the table.
"The Senate convenes at noon in the Curia," Caecilius said. "The urban prefect will require this intelligence before the senators debate the grain distributions. An open war in the north will divert the Rhine legions and strain the Italic supply roads."
"The previous transmittal to Antioch went by ordinary post," Vettius said.
"This dispatch changes the priority," Caecilius said. "Draft an urgent imperial packet for Antioch. Inform the chancellery that the Limes Germanicus is engaged. State that two frontier posts have fallen to tribal assaults across Germania Superior and Raetia, and the barbarian war-bands are testing the legionary lines. Append the full text from Castra Regina and the preliminary Lorch report again."
"Under whose authority?" Vettius asked.
"Endorse it under the joint seal of the Urban Prefecture and the Senate," Caecilius ordered. "Instruct the courier master to send it by accelerated relay to Brundisium. A fast military galley must carry it across the sea to Seleucia Pieria. The Emperor must have this in Antioch with every possible speed."

29 — Don't Dawdle
Winter had set in along the Kocher, and the stream in the hollow ran under a thin layer of ice. Under the limestone ledge, Tove had a small, smokeless fire going between flat stones, heating strips of salted meat.
Toria sat near the coals, flexing her fingers.
"It barely pulls now," Toria said. "I could hold a blade today."
"You will still take it easy, my dear," Tove said, turning the meat with her knife. "We could try a bit of sparring to see if you can swing iron in the cold without splitting the scab."
"She'll be alright, Tove," Thana said. "The locals aren't likely to put up much of a fight anyway. Thilda and I had to listen to two teamsters at the lower ford yesterday swearing an entire war-band of forest ghosts is haunting the road."
"They were shouting at their oxen like men possessed," Thilda said. "One swore he'd seen thirty horsemen in the mist, and the other swore it was fifty. Neither of them had actually seen anything, but they'd heard about the dead horses outside Lorch and decided every tree was armed."
Thana laughed. "And Schirenhof?"
"Barred tight," said Taran. "I watched from the ridge yesterday at midday. Two woodcutters came down with a cart and had to hammer on the oak timbers for half an hour before a sentry would even poke his nose over the wall. They wouldn't open the gate. The sentry lowered a basket on a rope to haul up their axes so they could prove they were Romans."
"Good," said Tiata. "A garrison that won't open its gates to its own woodcutters is doing our work for us."
Just then, Tigratavā dropped down through the cleft in the rock.
"Movement," she said. "On the valley track, heading north towards this ridge."
Tiata stood. "How many?"
"Eight," Tigratavā said. "Heavy cavalry from Aalen, riding two abreast at an easy walk. They left the fort about five minutes ago. I watched them to see which way they were headed, and their path brings them right past our hollow."
"We can't have that," Tove said. "We're too settled for the winter."
"We need to take them on the lower bend," said Thilda. "The rock shelf pinches the track there—hazel on the right, the drop into the creek on the left. The ground is frozen hard, and they won't be able to turn."
"That sounds perfect," said Tiata. "We'll go down there and greet them."
⁂
The trail bent sharply around a shoulder of limestone about half a mile below the crest. On the uphill side, bare hazel and birch hung out over the path. On the downhill side, the ground dropped twenty feet into frozen briars leading to the creek bed.
Decurion Marcius rode at the head of the file. He wore his scale cuirass over a thick red tunic, his crested helmet buckled tight beneath his chin. His eyes were dark with exhaustion, but he sat his bay stallion with the ease of twenty years in the saddle. Behind him, seven troopers rode single file as they passed through the narrow defile, their cloaks pinned at the shoulder, round shields resting against their thighs.
At the narrowest part of the bend, Marcius drew rein, his breath misting before him as he scanned the quiet timber ahead.
A heavy trilobate struck the bronze girth buckle, shearing the plate clean through. A second shaft from a shelf above buried its point into the stallion's rump.
The bay squealed and reared; the four-horned saddle tore loose and slid backwards over the tail. Marcius lost his balance and tumbled backwards, landing flat on his back in the frozen bracken with a thud that knocked the wind from his lungs.
The horse kicked out, iron shoes clattering on the rime, and bolted up the track into the timber.
Marcius rolled over and got to his knees, but before he could rise to his feet, the cold edge of a gladius pressed against his throat.
At that moment, the forest erupted.
Tiata appeared through the brush, swinging the falx to take out the forelegs of the leading two mounts. Both beasts collapsed forwards with shrill, wet screams, throwing their riders into the rocks. Tove followed, her axe shattering the skull of a third mount, then sweeping back to take the rider.
Taran dropped from the shelf above onto the shoulders of an auxiliary struggling to unsheath his sword, knocking him to the ground. Thilda stepped into the path with her framea, thrusting it into the breast of a rearing mare, sending it stumbling backwards down the slope into the briars, while Thana darted under a panicked horse to thrust upward with her xiphos.
Even Toria took down a horse and rider with a few swings of the falcata.
It was over in under a minute.
The thrashing died into the wet rasp of dying mounts and the twitching of boots in the frost.
Marcius remained kneeling, Tacita's blade biting into his skin. He had watched seven men and their mounts butchered before his eyes.
Thana stepped over a dead mount and crouched beside Marcius with a thin smirk.
"You really shouldn't ride in the woods in winter, Decurion," she said. "The frost makes the leather so brittle."
She unbuckled his sword-belt, drew his spatha, and tossed it down the twenty-foot drop into the creek. She pulled his pugio from his hip and flicked it across the track, burying it deep into a tree trunk.
"A decurion of the Ala," Thana murmured. "Such fine scales. But not very good at keeping your seat, are you?"
Tiata stepped up, resting the butt of her falx on the turf. "Get him up."
Tacita eased the blade back a finger's breadth, caught him by the shoulder-guard, and pulled him to his feet.
Tove walked over with a length of stiff rawhide.
"Hands behind your back, lad," Tove said cheerfully. "Don't make me break cold fingers."
Marcius, trembling so hard his scales rattled, brought his wrists together behind his back. Tove bound them tight, pulling the leather until his knuckles went pale.
"Leave his armour on," Taran said. "He might need some protection if he trips on his way home."
Tiata spun him by the shoulders so he was facing down the track.
"Go," she said, thrusting him between the shoulders.
Marcius stumbled forwards, then regained his footing and began his descent.
"Off you trot now, Decurion," Tove called after him. "Don't dawdle."

30 — Defiance
The north watch caught sight of him around midday, where the valley track came out of the hazel scrub.
The sentry on the gate tower watched the figure for several minutes before calling down into the yard. At that distance, it looked like a carter or a straggler from one of the outposts, moving slowly along the frozen ruts. Then the sun caught the crest of a helmet and the iron scale of an officer's cuirass.
Tribune Valerius was crossing from the armoury workshops when the call came down. He climbed the rampart stair without hurrying, took the sentry's place at the railing, and looked north.
The man was three hundred paces out. He was stumbling awkwardly, and his hands were pulled tight behind his back.
Valerius recognised him immediately.
"Hold the gate," Valerius told the sentry. "Do not raise the alarm."
He went down the stairs and headed directly to the north portal. He ordered the watch to slide the oak wicket back just wide enough for him to pass, then he squeezed through, and met Marcius as he approached the gate.
The decurion's face was grey with frost and exhaustion, and his hands had gone white from the tension of the rawhide.
Valerius cut the binding, and helped him through the wicket.
"Keep your hands under your cloak," Valerius said quietly. "Keep your head down, and walk beside me."
Valerius motioned the gate guards back to their posts, and led Marcius down the narrow lane behind the granary wall to the principia.
They entered the record room off the basilica, and Valerius shut the heavy oak door behind them.
Decurion Senecio was at the table, checking an inventory of fodder. He looked up, took in the absence of Marcius's sword belt, the grey pallor of his skin, and the raw red creases across his wrists, and laid his stylus down.
Marcius sat down and gave them a full account. He recalled pausing at the narrow defile before being thrown, saddle and all, from his mount, and scrambling to his knees only to be halted by a blade at his throat and a hoarse "Don't!" in his ear. He recounted watching as the other seven horses were mercilessly cut down under their riders, and how the seven troopers were ruthlessly dispatched in cold blood. He told of six women who had stepped from the brush to disarm him, and the callous way they had dismissed him.
When he finished, the room remained silent for a long time.
Valerius walked to the window. Outside, the yard was quiet, troopers leading horses to the water troughs in orderly pairs.
"You need to stay in here, Marcius," Valerius said, turning from the sill.
Marcius looked up. "Custody, sir?"
"Not through any misdemeanour of yours," Valerius said. "We cannot afford to have the men hearing that six or seven women butchered a mounted patrol barely a mile from this gate and sent you home with your wrists tied. You need to stay out of sight for now."
"A full turma," Senecio said. "Thirty heavy horse. We must ride up that gorge, recover our dead, and comb the northeast ridges until we find them."
"No," Valerius said.
Senecio looked at him. "Seven troopers from this garrison are lying in the snow just over a mile from our gate."
"And if you ride thirty heavy horse into that same rocky gorge, you will give them thirty more carcasses to butcher," Valerius replied, his tone dry and cold. "Have you forgotten Dalkingen? Have you forgotten the defile at Böbingen? They choose narrow ground because our cavalry cannot form. The moment your column enters that brush, your mounts will be hamstrung in the rocks, and you will achieve nothing."
"Then we dismount and sweep the ridge on foot."
"I won't allow it," Valerius said.
"We cannot leave seven Roman soldiers to rot in the brush." Senecio took a step towards the table. "You sent a dispatch to Castra Regina telling the Legatus that the limes was quiet and the disturbances were minor. You told him the Third Legion could sit on the Danube. Now our senior decurion wanders home on foot like a stray mule, and you want to sit behind stone and pretend it never happened."
Valerius met Senecio's gaze with a cold stare. "I told the Legatus what the provincial administration needed to hear. An officer does not panic the high command over rumours and broken patrols."
"You lied to save your standing," Senecio said, his voice rising, "and you are hiding behind these walls because you cannot explain what stands outside them. I am acting commander of this Ala. The tactical disposition of these twenty-four turmae is my responsibility."
"You hold acting command by my appointment," Valerius said coldly. "I hold the seal of Legio III Italica and the mandate of the governor by extension. My authority over this sector is clear. If you order a squadron out of that gate against my judgement, I will relieve you of command immediately and place you under arrest for insubordination."
The two men stood across the cedar boards, the lamplight falling between them.
"You can relieve me when I return," Senecio said, turning on his heel and leaving the chamber.
"Senecio," Valerius called after him. "Stand fast."
Senecio did not check his stride. He crossed the flagged floor of the basilica, pushed through the bronze doors, and walked out onto the principia steps.
"Cornicularius," Senecio called down into the yard.
The duty clerk came running from the administrative office.
"Sound the muster for the Third Turma."

31 — Foolish Lads
They made their way back to the hollow after watching Marcius begin his solitary return.
Toria stood by the frozen stream, opening and closing her hand.
"It didn't pull once," she said, looking down at her arm. "Not even when that bastard tried to block me."
Tove came over and peeled back the linen to inspect the skin.
"It's looking good," she said, smoothing the cloth back down. "Just don't go trying anything too heroic."
"We really can't stay here," said Tiata. "Marcius will be at the north gate by noon, and they'll know we're up here somewhere."
Taran looked up from checking her bow. "I'd like to check in on Lorch's northern neighbours."
Tiata turned to her. "The Upper Germanic line?"
"The garrisons north of the Knee," Taran said. "The Praefectus at Lorch was always complaining about them. Said they were undermanned and half-asleep, and that Rome was wasting good grain keeping them fed."
Thilda nodded. "The country north of the Knee is broken forest. I know the tracks through the high ground. We can bypass Aalen completely if we take the limestone ridge above the Kocher. The cavalry can't follow us up there very easily."
They cleared the camp quickly. Tove kicked dirt over the hearth stones, and they led the five horses up the rocky cleft towards the crest.
At the ridge, Tigratavā paused to look back down into the valley.
"It looks like they're sending out a full turma," she said. "And a couple of wagons for the dead."
"They're in for a cold, fruitless afternoon," said Tove.
They turned the horses and kept moving.
⁂
Senecio led the cavalry up the same path the others had taken that morning, and soon found the scene of carnage.
Senecio dismounted. "First squad, load the dead onto the wagons," he said. "The rest of you, scour the ridge. They will have left tracks somewhere up there."
Ten men climbed up into the scrub on foot, shields slung, working their way up the rock shelves.
Senecio waited by the track while the troopers loaded the bodies. It was cold, and the dead were already stiffening in the frost.
Two hours passed before the scouts came back down.
The optio in charge shook his head. "Nothing, sir. We found a hollow half a mile up, but there's no camp. No fire, no tether marks, no gear left behind. Just cold rock."
"Tracks?" Senecio asked.
"The ground above is solid limestone, so there's nothing to follow."
Senecio looked at the dead men in the wagons. "Form up," he said. "We're going back."
⁂
It was late afternoon when the turma rode back into Aalen.
At the gate, another party was already mounted. Tribune Valerius was at the head of the six remaining legionaries he had brought from Castra Regina.
He pulled up when Senecio's column blocked the passage.
For a moment neither man spoke. Senecio sat his horse, looking at Valerius, with the seven bodies clearly visible behind him.
Valerius looked away from the dead.
"A courier arrived from Castra Regina," Valerius said. "Legatus Flaccus has requested my return. Administrative matters on the Danube."
Senecio looked at him for a long moment. He understood well enough what was happening. There had been no courier — Valerius simply couldn't face the truth. What he would tell Flaccus when he reached Castra Regina, Senecio couldn't imagine, but whatever it was would be far from the truth.
"Ride safe, Tribune," Senecio said.
He reined his mount aside, and the troopers behind him parted to let the legionaries through.
Valerius rode past without another word, his six men following, and headed for the open road.
⁂
A few miles to the west, five horses were keeping to the high ground above the road.
Thilda led the way along the crest, well out of sight of the valley floor. As they passed Böbingen, they could see the gate was barred and the sentry on the tower was standing alone in his cloak, watching the empty road.
Shortly after, they skirted Schirenhof, looking much the same as Böbingen.
Beyond Schirenhof, the road dropped towards the dark valley where Lorch lay empty, the way open into the deep forests of Upper Germania.
Tove looked back towards line of Raetian forts, shaking her head.
"Foolish lads," she said, with a quiet laugh. "They outdo themselves. At this rate they'll have the whole empire down around their ears."

32 — General Alarm
Two weeks after the collapse of the gate at Dalkingen, the courier from Aalen rode through the west gate of Castra Regina. Legatus Quintus Caecilius Flaccus sat in the basilica with Camp Prefect Calpurnius when the dispatch reached the table. The sheet bore the official seal of Tribune Valerius. Flaccus cracked the wax and read the lines in the morning light.
The dispatch stated that the limes stood firm and the stone wall remained intact. There had been a few losses along the roads, but they represented isolated friction between native settlements. Valerius affirmed that the Ala held the sector in hand, and the two cohorts of the Third Legion could remain in their quarters along the Danube.
Calpurnius took the sheet, his eyes scanning the summary.
"Senecio holds the command together," Calpurnius said. "The cavalry maintains the border, and the timber roads remain open."
"Valerius handles his duties with proper discipline," Flaccus said, placing the letter into the provincial archive box. "He restores order to an auxiliary garrison and prevents false alarms."
⁂
The following afternoon, seven horses clattered across the flagstones of the west gate. Tribune Valerius rode into the courtyard at the head of the six legionary troopers he had taken to Aalen. Clay caked the horses to their withers, and the riders moved with stiff, aching limbs.
Calpurnius watched the arrival from the principia portico. Valerius dismounted, his boots striking the cobbles with a heavy, stumbling gait. His skin showed grey beneath the dried silt of the road, and his eyes stayed fixed on the gravel.
"Tribune," Calpurnius said. "Your dispatch arrived yesterday."
"I must report to the Legatus," Valerius said, his voice hoarse and thin.
"Flaccus attends the cavalry stables," Calpurnius said. "You look spent from the saddle."
"The road was hard," Valerius murmured. "I require rest before I speak."
He surrendered his reins to a groom and walked toward the staff quarters, keeping his gaze fixed on the flagstones.
At dawn the following morning, Flaccus summoned Valerius to the record room behind the basilica. Calpurnius stood beside the window with the provincial map laid out on the cedar boards. Valerius entered in a clean tunic, yet his face retained a sickly grey cast. Tremors shook his fingers as he stood before the table.
Flaccus leaned forwards, his hands resting flat on the timber.
"Your written report described a quiet frontier, Tribune," Flaccus said, his voice even. "You stated the Ala held the sector and the only trouble was minor friction among farmers. Yet you rode through my gates twenty-four hours behind your own courier, leaving Senecio alone with a thousand horses. Explain your presence in Castra Regina."
Valerius swallowed hard. He clutched his belt with shaking hands.
"The written report preserved public order, Legatus," Valerius said, his breath coming fast. "The reality on the limes is slaughter."
Flaccus straightened, his brow furrowing.
"Speak plainly," Flaccus ordered.
"Alemannic war-bands occupy the entire timber belt between the Rems and the frontier wall," Valerius said, his voice rising. "They strike from deep cover in the rocky defiles. They ambush every patrol. They butcher the cavalry mounts at the hock, severing the hamstrings with heavy blades and leaving the horses screaming in the rocks. They drop from overhead branches onto mounted troopers. They slaughter whole squads and leave the dead to rot in the brush."
"And the Ala?" Calpurnius demanded. "A thousand heavy horse?"
"Paralyzed," Valerius said, sweat glistening on his temples. "Senecio keeps the squadrons behind the stone ramparts because the mounts are butchered the moment they enter the trees. Buch bars its gates at midday. Böbingen and Schirenhof keep their gates shut and abandon the road. The outposts are cut off and bleeding every morning. I rode south to deliver the true scale of the disaster before the enemy surrounds Aalen."
The room fell into heavy quiet. Flaccus studied the young officer. Valerius stood swaying on his heels, his chest heaving, his fingers digging into his palms. Dread filled the tribune's face.
Flaccus saw a dedicated staff officer broken by the horror of savage warfare. He viewed the shaking limbs and hollow stare as the physical toll of extreme combat strain. In his eyes, Valerius had endured the terrors of a crumbling border while Raeticus lay dying and auxiliary commanders succumbed to panic. Flaccus felt profound sympathy for the man. Here stood a tribune who had fulfilled his duty in a violent province and possessed the fortitude to bring the brutal truth in person.
Flaccus stepped around the table and placed a firm hand on Valerius's shoulder.
"You endured severe strain at Aalen, Valerius," Flaccus said, his voice softening with respect. "You held the command steady while Raeticus collapsed, and you brought the truth through hostile roads. That required true virtus."
Valerius blinked, tears wetting his eyes as he absorbed the praise.
"Rest in your quarters, Tribune," Flaccus added kindly. "You served the legion well. The medics will tend to you."
Valerius bowed his head, offered a quiet salute, and walked out into the corridor.
Flaccus turned to Calpurnius, his expression severe.
"Raetia faces an organized barbarian invasion," Flaccus said. "The Alemanni are systematically destroying our auxiliary outposts and crippling our cavalry."
"Shall I assemble the cohorts?" Calpurnius asked.
"Place the First and Second Cohorts under arms immediately," Flaccus commanded. "Issue field rations, check the draught oxen, and prepare the baggage train for a forced march to the Brenz junction."
"The chancellery must receive the full operational reality," Flaccus said. "I will draft the general alarm for the Senate and the imperial court."

33 — Panic in Rome
One month after the fall of Lorch, the official dispatch from Moguntiacum reached the Tabularium in Rome. Vettius received the leather pouch at his desk, breaking the green seal of the governor of Germania Superior. The contents overturned the earlier assessment. The forwarded sheets contained the formal testimony of the Lorch garrison, setting down the operational disaster in full detail.
Vettius read the casualty lists, his brow furrowing as the numbers climbed. The report detailed an overwhelming Alemannic invasion force breaking across the frontier wall. Hundreds of auxiliaries lay dead in the ashes of the fort. The cavalry mounts were butchered in their stalls. Eighty mangled survivors had reached the Rhine on farm carts, their wounds rotting from the winter frost. Centurion Marcellus sat struck dumb by combat shock, his mind broken by the slaughter.
Vettius brought the dispatch directly to Caecilius. Caecilius read every line, his face turning pale. The memory of his earlier mockery returned to him.
"This was an actual siege," Caecilius said, his voice flat.
"A general tribal assault," Vettius replied. "The Praefectus retreated to preserve the three hundred men still capable of bearing arms."
Caecilius laid the report onto the central table.
"A permanent stone fort destroyed," Caecilius said. "The Rhine command has pulled back to the river."
Seven days later, a second courier reached the intake desk from the north. The rider wore the badges of Legio III Italica, his cloak caked in white road dust from the Alpine passes. He delivered the general alarm from Legatus Flaccus at Castra Regina.
Vettius spread the papyrus before Caecilius. The Raetian report confirmed the expanding crisis. Flaccus reported that the Alemanni had breached the frontier defenses and were hunting inside the province. The thousand cavalry of the Ala II Flavia remained confined behind the stone walls of Aalen. Patrols were butchered in rocky gorges. The auxiliary outposts at Buch, Böbingen, and Schirenhof kept their gates barred at midday, cut off from the main road. Flaccus had ordered two cohorts of the Third Legion under arms and requested immediate imperial mobilization along the Danube.
The reality stood visible across the maps. Two separate commanders, divided by provincial boundaries and administrative rivalries, described the same coordinated collapse. Germania Superior reported its frontier anchor burned and evacuated. Raetia reported its elite cavalry crippled and its outposts surrounded.
Panic spread through the administrative quarters. Word reached the Forum before noon. The urban prefect called an emergency session of the Senate at the Curia. Senators gathered in the hall, their speeches filled with alarm over the open northern border.
Caecilius prepared the imperial packet for the East. He bundled the Moguntiacum casualty manifests with Flaccus's general alarm and the urgent appeals of the Senate. He rolled the heavy collection into a lead-lined cylinder, sealing the cap with the red wax of the urban prefecture.

34 — Murrhardt
The ridge above Murrhardt gave a clear view of the frontier ditch. Deep snow blanketed the northern slope, clinging to the spruce boughs and weighting the birch thickets along the boundary apron. Down in the valley, the split-oak palisade ran between two timber watchtowers, its sharpened stakes iced over in the midwinter frost.
A blast from an aurochs horn echoed from the eastern timber. Shouts carried across the cleared apron, followed by the crash of heavy boots breaking through frozen crust. Over a hundred Alemannic warriors burst into the open ground, brandishing iron axes, framea spears, and burning pitch brands. They ran straight for the palisade in a dense mass, their war cries rolling up the valley.
The auxiliary watchtower came alive with frantic movement. Two Roman sentries scrambled to the timber railing, loosing arrows into the advancing crowd. Three raiders took shafts in the chest and tumbled into the ditch, their blood staining the trampled snow. The charge kept moving over the fallen bodies. Warriors threw heavy scaling hooks over the split oak, while half a dozen men attacked the wooden palisade with felling axes, wood chips flying across the clay rampart.
The sentries on the platform threw javelins into the crush below, killing two more axemen, but the defense was hurried and chaotic. Pitch brands arced through the freezing air, landing on the dry shingles of the tower roof. Flames caught immediately, fed by the resinous spruce timbers. Below the catwalk, the oak stakes gave way under the repeated blows, splintering inward with a loud snap.
Alemanni swarmed through the breach, howling and hacking at the tower supports. The garrison inside the lower gatehouse tried to form a shield wall, but the mass of warriors overwhelmed the threshold. Iron blades rose and fell in a brutal clash. Auxiliaries died against the stone footings while their roof collapsed in a shower of burning thatch. The survivors fled south toward the main fort at Murrhardt, running on foot through the snow. The raiders plundered the storehouse, hauling out cured hams, bronze kettles, and bundles of cohort blankets, cheering around the blazing ruins while twelve of their own men lay dead in the ditch.
In the spruce cover on the ridge, eight women watched the smoke rise into the grey sky.

35 — Antioch
Emperor Marcus Aurelius Severus Alexander had held the purple for twelve years, elevated as a youth of fourteen after the Praetorian Guard killed his cousin Elagabalus in Rome. His mother, Julia Mamaea, directed the government of the empire. Holding the title of Augusta, she managed the treasury, selected the provincial governors, and controlled access to the throne. Alexander deferred to her judgement in all decisions. He had grown to manhood inside palace corridors, accustomed to silk garments, warm baths, and soft Syrian winters. For two years, the court had occupied the imperial palace at Antioch, overseeing the Persian war through dispatches while the army endured the campaign in Mesopotamia. Alexander dreaded the rough company of soldiers, trembling whenever military delegations entered the audience hall.
Midwinter brought heavy, steady rain to the Levant. The Orontes river flooded the pastures along the valley floor, turning the military cantonments outside Antioch into deep fields of red mud. Cold dampness seeped through the wet wool and saturated leather of the legionary tents, rotting kit and bringing fever to the ranks.
Inside the palace on the river island, charcoal braziers heated the private chambers with burning cedar and frankincense. Thick tapestries covered the limestone walls, blocking the draft. Alexander sat beside the coals in a silk robe lined with Persian sable, shivering whenever the gale rattled the shutters.
The first news of the unrest on the Limes Germanicus arrived from Rome by courier galley through Seleucia Pieria.
Julia Mamaea took the cylinder from the messenger, breaking the chancellery seal. She read the report from the Tabularium detailing the evacuation of Fort Lorch.
Alexander stared at his mother, his hands shaking against his fur trim.
"The Germanic frontier has broken," Alexander said, tears welling in his eyes. "The barbarians will cross the Alps."
"A single fort suffered a fire," Mamaea replied, tossing the papyrus onto an ebony table. "The local commander fled his own shadows. The provincial governor has sufficient auxiliary cohorts to rebuild a burned stable. The court remains in Syria to conclude the Persian treaties."
Alexander wiped his cheeks with his silk sleeve and pulled his feet closer to the brazier.
⁂
Ten days later, as the rain fell in torrents across the valley, a second courier from Rome reached the palace, escorted by palace guards.
The dispatch carried the joint seal of the Senate and the Urban Prefecture, enclosing the report from Castra Regina.
Mamaea spread the sheet before the imperial council. The news was grave. An Alemannic war-band had destroyed the gate at Dalkingen, killed the auxiliary detachment, and battered down the bronze monument of Caracalla. The Ala at Aalen had marched in full strength, finding only smoldering ruins.
Alexander gripped the arms of his ivory chair, his voice rising in panic.
"Raetia is burning," Alexander wept. "The tribes are inside the province! We must send gold. We must buy peace before they reach Rome!"
Mamaea scowled at her son, her fingers tightening on the dispatch.
"We buy peace when it suits our treasury," Mamaea said sharply. "Raetia has eight hundred legionaries at Castra Regina and a thousand heavy horse at Aalen. They can patrol their own woods. Our legions stay here until the eastern borders are secure."
⁂
The army stationed around Antioch held thousands of veterans from the Rhine and Danube legions, transferred east two years earlier for the Persian war. They lived in waterlogged tents, their boots caked in thick clay and their armor rusting in the damp air.
A third packet of dispatches arrived ten days after the second, carried by an imperial relay that had ridden hard from Brundisium.
This packet contained the formal casualty report from Moguntiacum and the general alarm from Legatus Flaccus. Lorch was destroyed, with hundreds of auxiliaries dead and the survivors huddled behind the Rhine. In Raetia, the cavalry was crippled, outposts were cut off, and barbarian war-bands roamed the interior defiles.
Word spread from the palace chancellery to the military camps before midday. Scribes and quartermasters passed the accounts down to the centurions, and on to the ranks.
Fury swept through the tents. Rhine legionaries threw aside their trenching tools and gathered in the muddy avenues of the camp. Their homelands were burning in the winter snow, their families were exposed to barbarian axes, and their Emperor kept them wallowing in Syrian mud to flatter eastern court officials.
The anger turned to mutiny.
Hundreds of armed legionaries marched through the rain to the palace gates, banging their spathas against their bronze shield bosses. Centurions who attempted to restore order were shoved aside in the red mud. The shouts of the soldiers echoed across the Orontes island, demanding an urgent march to the West and threatening to proclaim a new emperor if their homes were abandoned to the tribes.
Inside the great hall, the clamor of the soldiers shook the palace guards.
Alexander collapsed onto a cushioned couch, sobbing openly into his hands, completely overwhelmed by the roaring crowd outside the walls.
"They will kill me," Alexander stammered, his body shaking with dread. "The soldiers will tear down the gates."
Julia Mamaea stepped between the Emperor and the cowering magistrates, her face set hard.
"Quiet yourself," Mamaea commanded her son.
She turned to the Praetorian prefect and the assembled tribunes.
"The Emperor has heard the will of the army," Mamaea said, her voice carrying across the marble hall. "We will march for the West."

36 — Soft Underbelly
Smoke from the fallen tower rose straight into the grey sky as the Alemanni vanished into the northern spruce, dragging their plunder over the frozen turf.
On the crest, Tigratavā lowered herself flat against the limestone shelf, watching the valley floor below.
"The main gates at Murrhardt are barred tight," Tigratavā said. "Every sentry stands on the north parapet, shields raised, watching the smoke."
"They expect the whole forest to follow," Thana said. "They are bracing for a war-band that's just run off with their breakfast."
"After losing Lorch, they're expecting the worst," said Thilda. "It wouldn't surprise me if they're like this all the way along the limes."
"Then I'd say we've achieved what we set out to achieve," Tove said. "They've rolled over, leaving their soft underbelly exposed. We can let the Alemanni tear them apart now, if they don't tear themselves apart first."
Thilda moved a bit further along, peering down through another gap.
"What have you found?" asked Tiata.
"Horses. Looks like a watering detail. There's a river pen below the south wall."
Tiata looked down at the river terrace. "How many horses are there?"
"Six," Thilda said. "And they're even saddled."
"Aren't the grooms with them?" asked Toria. "Or did they all run like rabbits?"
Thana laughed. "They didn't all run like rabbits," she said. "Some ran like hairy goats."
"No, I saw them run when the horn blew," said Tigratavā.
"All we need is another three sound mounts," said Tiata. "Then we can travel a lot easier."
They led their five mounts down the gully through the dwarf spruce, the limestone banks hiding their descent. At the valley floor, the Murr river ran over grey boulders, fringed by dense alder and willow scrub.
Upstream, Fort Murrhardt sat on its low river terrace. From inside the stone curtain came the blare of horns and the shouting of decurions forming ranks along the north walkway.
"We'll need to be quick," said Tigratavā. "It looks like they're going to be coming out shortly."
Just ahead of them, the log rails of the paddock held six sturdy auxiliary horses, their winter coats rough against the cold air.
Tove, Toria and Tacita moved cautiously into the open ground, while the others waited out of sight.
The three women moved along the frozen mud of the riverbank, keeping low below the lip of the terrace. They reached the timber rails and climbed through. Tove approached the nearest three, murmuring softly. The horses stood calmly as Tacita gathered the reins.
Toria lifted the drop-bar of the paddock gate, holding the oak beam firmly with both hands as the iron pins cleared the brackets.
They led the three horses through the gap and guided them into the willow brush, leaving the pen open behind them.
Each woman now held the reins of her own mount. They swung up into their saddles, and rode back up to the ridge.
The breach opened twenty paces wide where the Alemanni had hacked through the oak. Around the breach, the frozen ditch held the bodies of the twelve fallen warriors, stripped of their weapons, lying among splintered oak posts and blood-stained rime.
The eight horses picked their way down the ditch bank, stepping over the split timbers, and climbed the far slope onto the boundary apron, through the shattered wall and on into the northern forest.
Toria glanced over her shoulder, where the noise of the Roman column could be heard approaching the breach in the palisade.
"It's a pity they caved so easily," she said. "I was hoping to join in the fun again once my arm healed."
"There's plenty more where they came from," said Thana. "In Dacia, they don't even keep them behind a wall."

Historical Note
The fort at Aalen housed the largest cavalry garrison on the Raetian limes, covering roughly fifteen acres as the base for the Ala II Flavia milliaria — an elite regiment of one thousand heavy cavalry. It stood on the military highway connecting Augusta Vindelicum (modern Augsburg) across the Danube to the frontier. The nearby fort at Buch, home to an auxiliary unit generally identified as Cohors III Thracum (a 500-strong part-mounted cohort), lay a little over a mile from the frontier and guarded the same approach.

The Limes Gate at Dalkingen is the only monument of its kind discovered along the entire Upper Germanic-Raetian frontier. Following Emperor Caracalla's Germanic campaign around 213 AD, an existing border gatehouse was transformed into an opulent triumphal arch of dressed sandstone. Over 140 fragments of a larger-than-life gilded bronze statue of an emperor in military cuirass — widely believed to depict Caracalla — were recovered from the site and are now preserved in the Limesmuseum in Aalen. Excavations confirm that the gate and its timber guardhouse were destroyed by fire and abandoned during third-century frontier turmoil, traditionally associated with the Alemannic invasion of 233–234 AD. Archaeological evidence also shows that the portal itself had been walled off and blocked prior to its destruction, ending its function as a gateway. Since 2010, the conserved ruins have stood protected under a striking glass-and-steel enclosure.

Moguntiacum (modern Mainz) was the provincial capital of Germania Superior and long-term base of Legio XXII Primigenia. Its legionary fortress, established under Augustus, remained in military use into the fourth century. Although the official provincial capital of Raetia was Augusta Vindelicum, the effective seat of military command lay at Castra Regina (modern Regensburg). Built in 179 AD as the base of Legio III Italica — the only legion stationed in the province — the fortress served as the headquarters of the governor, who held both military and civil authority. Its northern portal, the Porta Praetoria, remains partially preserved in the old town — surviving alongside Trier's Porta Nigra as one of the few standing Roman stone gate structures in Germany.

The wider collapse of the frontier depicted across this book is historical, known as the Limesfall. The fort and palisade at Murrhardt sat along the Outer Limes of Upper Germania, where an earthen rampart, a split-oak palisade, and timber watchtowers guarded the frontier line. Although the permanent abandonment of the trans-Rhenish frontier territory (the Agri Decumates) culminated around 259–260 AD, massive Alemannic incursions began in earnest in 233 AD, burning and destroying outposts, watchtowers, and supply routes across Raetia and Upper Germany.

That unrest did not stay contained to the frontier. Emperor Severus Alexander’s war against the Sassanid king Ardashir I had kept him, his mother Julia Mamaea, and much of the Rhine and Danube legions stationed in the East since 231 AD. That Persian campaign had wound down without a decisive victory by late 233 AD, against the backdrop of alarming dispatches arriving from the northern governors. In 234 AD, the crisis compelled the imperial court to abandon Syria and march north to establish field headquarters at Moguntiacum to confront the Germanic threat. The logistical unraveling of this expedition and the final collapse of the Severan dynasty are detailed in the Historical Note of the sequel to this book.

Today, the Upper Germanic-Raetian frontier is preserved as part of the UNESCO World Heritage "Frontiers of the Roman Empire".




---




Book 3

Limesgruppe
The Ghosts of the Empire 

1 — North of the Wall
Deep snow had settled over the Swabian ridges before the year died, burying the tracks in knee-deep drifts and weighting the spruce boughs until they brushed the horses’ withers. Moving eight mounts through the timber meant keeping below the crests where the wind stripped the stone bare, picking a slow line through frozen birch and hazel scrub.
"We're barely making ten miles a day," Toria said, looking back over her shoulder. "A crippled ox moves faster than this."
"A crippled horse doesn't move at all," Tove said from the saddle behind her. "Let them pick their footing. If a mare breaks a leg in a hole, you're the one carrying the tack."
"No Roman is out on these roads in this weather," Thana said from the bay ahead. "Be glad of the snow. It saves having to look over our shoulders."
"It's too quiet," Toria muttered.
Up ahead, Thilda checked her mount where the timber thinned against a shelf of weathered rock, and the rest pulled up behind her.
Ahead, the forest sloped gently south towards the cleared apron of the Raetian border. Through the bare trees, the ruins of Dalkingen sat fifty paces below.
Three months had done little to soften the wreckage. Snow had drifted over the collapsed sandstone arch, turning the imperial monument into an ugly mound of yellow rubble jutting from the ditch. Six feet of crushed ashlar choked the road, the toppled bronze emperor buried somewhere beneath the ice and stone.
On the catwalk above the wall, two auxiliaries huddled under a wicker windbreak, their cloaks pulled over their ears, staring into a three-legged iron brazier between their boots. Neither looked north.
Taran leaned forwards over her pommel, watching them through the bare birch twigs. Her eyes narrowed, and a slow, wicked twitch touched the corner of her mouth.
"They look cold," she said softly.
Toria caught the look and grinned. "You're like a Cupid from the underworld."
Taran was already sliding the yew bow from her back. She laid a heavy broadhead against the riser, drew smoothly to the corner of her jaw, and loosed.
The shaft cleared the lower spruce boughs with a soft hiss and dropped sixty paces down the slope.
The heavy iron broadhead struck the rim of the brazier with a sharp, ringing crack.
The tripod buckled, and the bowl flipped sideways. A shovel-load of red-hot charcoal and glowing ash dumped straight into the lap of the nearest sentry, spilling across his greasy wool cloak and his companion's boots.
The man let out a screech that cut through the frozen woods like a stuck pig. He threw himself backwards off his stool, rolling frantically in the slush on the walkway and slapping at his smoking groin. The second guard slid around precariously on the icy planks, trying to kick glowing coals off the edge and swearing at the empty air.
Above in the tree line, Taran chuckled softly.
"They look warmer now," she said.
Toria shook with quiet laughter, and even Tacita's eyes crinkled in the shadow of her hood. They turned the horses together, slipping along the contour of the ridge, leaving the two sentries dancing on the catwalk.
⁂
They tracked the frontier for two weeks, staying in the tree line north of the wall.
The white limestone ran straight across the country, four paces high, ignoring the hills and ravines. Every thousand paces a stone tower stood above the ditch, its catwalk empty, smoke leaking from the roof shingles as the sentries burned whatever dry wood they had left.
The tribal lands to the north were dead quiet. The summer villages were buried in drifts, the livestock penned into the back of the roundhouses, the paths wiped clean by the wind. They crossed the frozen trough of a wolf pack heading south towards the game trails. Further east, they held the horses flat in a spruce hollow while five hunters drifted along a ridge above them without ever looking down.
Near Hienheim, the country opened out onto grey, wind-scoured flats, and the limestone wall ended.
The stone simply ran down a low bluff into the frozen shallows of the Danube, and stopped.
Taran pulled her mount up, squinting through the glare at the dead masonry. "Did they run out of stone?"
"The river takes over here," Thilda said. "Rome doesn't build a wall when water can do the work."
"It's not doing much work today," Toria said, looking down at the vast, white plain of ice. "They forgot about winter."
"It will in a month," Tiata said. "Until then, it's open ground."
They swung away from the dead wall, pushing northeast into the skirts of the Bohemian Forest.
The spruce closed in, dense and black against the granite hills. They kept well north of the river, bypassing the fortress at Castra Regina. From the ridges above the Regen, they could see the haze of coal and woodsmoke hanging over the valley where the Third Legion sat in its barracks.
By this stage, the deep continental freeze had set in.
Every evening, Tigratavā saw to the mounts with the steady routine of the steppe, picking ice from their frogs and wrapping their lower legs in coarse wool against the snow-gall.
On the fourteenth day past the Regen, the granite bluffs broke open.
They dismounted and led the horses down through a narrow, boulder-strewn gully until the trees thinned.
Below them lay the gorge.
Two hundred feet down, three rivers met in a frozen basin. The Ilz came down from the northern rocks, dark and narrow. The Danube swept in wide from the west, its broad current locked under two feet of black ice dusted with powdered snow. And cutting in from the south was the Inn, its milky Alpine waters frozen into a jagged, uneven highway dividing Raetia from Noricum.
Across the expanse, sitting on the tongue of land between the rivers, was Boiodurum.
"The near bank is Raetia," Thilda said. "Across the bridge, with the wharves and the stone warehouse, is Noricum."
"And the fort?" Tiata asked.
"Down by the bridgehead," Thilda said. "Auxiliary foot. A few cavalry for the road, but mostly men minding gates."
The Danube below was dead silent. The cargo cranes on the wharves stood bare, their ropes stiff with rime. The timber barriers across the river gates were barred shut, and the stone towers along the water wall showed only thin curls of brazier smoke.
Tacita looked down at the ice, then caught Tiata's eye, raising an eyebrow.
Tiata responded with a nod. "I think I know just what you're thinking," she said. "Tigratavā, you've taken horses across the frozen Danube before. What's the best way to avoid detection?"
Tigratavā dropped to her knees beside her horse, pulling bundles of coarse burlap from her saddle roll. "We bind the hooves," she said. "Three turns around the iron. It muffles the stone, and it bites into the ice."
They worked without talking, kneeling in the drift. When they finished, the eight horses moved with a soft, muffled rustle, their iron shoes deadened against the rock.
They waited for the hours past midnight.
The moon slipped behind the western hills, leaving the gorge washed in blue starlight. The air was dead still.
Tigratavā took the lead down the switchbacks on foot. At the riverbank, the shore-ice groaned beneath their weight—a dull, hollow sound that echoed off the granite cliffs—but the crust held.
They stepped out onto the Danube together.
The crossing was fifty paces of open, wind-packed snow over iron-hard ice. Underfoot, the frozen river boomed occasionally as the current pushed beneath the crust, the sound vibrating up through their boots. The horses walked quietly, stepping in each other's tracks.

2 — The Cold Ledger
They made their camp in a wooded ravine high on the bluffs above the Inn, hidden in deadfall and scrub.
A shelf of rock kept the river wind off the bedrolls. Tove blocked the open side with packed snow and pine boughs, keeping their charcoal fire small and low so it gave off neither smoke nor flame. With eight horses to feed, they stripped scrub and melted snow by turns, never showing themselves on the skyline.
They spent the first two weeks working the station in pairs, trading watches from before dawn until well after dark.
Tigratavā and Thilda took the low ground, crawling through the frozen willow scrub along the river to read the timber and the water approach. Thana and Tacita held the bluffs, watching the road gate and timing the sentries. Higher up, Taran kept her eyes on the stone house, tracking movement behind the shutters whenever the smoke cleared.
When they gathered by the coals at night, the pieces came together quickly.
"Three guards on the road," Thana said. "They hate the wind. One man stands by the turnpike until his knees go stiff, then he bangs on the shack door and the next man takes his place. The road is clear for twenty paces while they swap."
"And the fort across the bridge?" Tiata asked.
"Fifty auxiliaries on foot," Thilda said. "You can see their sentries on the river wall, but they stay behind their own ditch. The customs post handles the road. Unless a courier sounds a horn, the bridge gate stays shut."
"What about the stone house?" Tiata asked, looking at Taran.
"Two clerks," Taran said. "A fat one in furs and a thin one at a desk. They have a charcoal pot between them, and the room is full of scrolls and wooden tablets. They don't look up, and they don't go outside. A boy brings them peat twice a day, and that's all."
"Why sit in the cold rewriting old paper?" Toria asked.
"Because they're terrified," Taran said. "I watched the Praefectus at Lorch rage about it for three winters. When the roads freeze, Rome stops fighting and starts counting. The clerks have to prove every bushel of grain and every copper coin taken in the province matches the duplicate records before the auditors arrive in the spring. If the numbers don't balance, people lose their pay or their heads. Those two down there aren't watching for an enemy. They're trying to save their own skins from their superiors."
"And the road?" Tiata asked.
