"Dead until the melt," Taran said. "Then it goes mad. The officers at Lorch talked about it every winter. The second the roads thaw, the orders come down to push fresh mounts and grain west to the Rhine for the spring campaign. Any herd coming down that highway from the east has to halt at this post. The drovers have to clear the papers before they're allowed across the bridge."
"And while they wait, the horses go in the lower pen," Tigratavā said.
"Tell us about the pen," Tiata said.
"Built on the gravel terrace below the road, right against the river," Tigratavā said. "Heavy post-and-rail oak. The road side is solid, but the river fence sits right on the drop into the shallows. That's where they put the troughs."
"So the horses face the water when they drink," Thilda noted.
"Their backs face the road," Tigratavā said. "A line of horses at that rail blocks the view from the stone house completely."
Tiata looked around the fire before she spoke again.
"If we rig that pen now while the ground is frozen and nobody's watching, when the thaw comes, the trap will be waiting."
⁂
The sabotage was done a couple of nights later.
A thick river fog came off the water, cold and wet, blotting out the road and the bluffs.
Tacita and Tigratavā went down after midnight.
They reached the riverbank from the water side, keeping low along the shelf where the shore-ice met the mud. Above them, the customs post was quiet, smelling of charcoal smoke and wet timber.
The holding pen sat dark and empty in the fog.
They worked the river fence from the shallows below the drop.
The rails were heavy oak, set into mortises in the upright posts and locked with square wooden pins. Tigratavā wedged a blade under the head of the first pin and eased it up. It came free with a dull scrape, and Tacita slid it out.
In its place, Tacita tapped in a green pine peg. She had carved it to match the square hole, but whittled the center down to the thickness of a straw. From above, the head looked flush and solid in the timber. Under the weight of a horse, it would snap instantly.
They moved along the five spans of the river rail. Ten oak pins pulled and packed into Tacita's pouch; ten weakened twigs tapped flush into the mortises.
At the center post, where the long rails met over the main trough, Tigratavā levered the rail up until it cleared the mortise lip, resting it on a loose river pebble.
One hard shove from inside, and the whole river span would fall into the water.
They were back up the bluffs before the fourth watch.
⁂
Two weeks later, the weather broke.
A warm wind blew in from the south, turning the hard frost into grey slush. Down in the gorge, the ice on the Danube began to crack with dull, heavy booms as the snowmelt came down out of the hills.
Tigratavā lay in the brush above the eastern road, watching through the bare branches.
She came back to the hollow before midday.
"Mounted men," she said. "Coming from the east."
Tiata climbed to the ledge with her.
A mile down the valley, moving at a steady walk through the mud, came the convoy. Eight riders in helmets and cloaks, leading thirty dark cavalry stallions hitched in neat strings of five.

3 — The Paperwork of the Empire
The paved highway approaching the Inn was a trench of yellow mud.
Gaius Valerius Vindex rode at the head of the file, his spatha slapping rhythmically against the skirts of his scale cuirass. Behind him, thirty Norican war-stallions stepped with heavy, sucking plops through the thawing clay, their breath rolling off their broad chests in thick grey clouds.
They had pushed forty miles in two days from the legionary depots at Lauriacum. The thaw had arrived with a vengeance, turning the packed snow into icy slush, and Vindex had no intention of losing another day on the road.
Ahead, where the highway met the timber bridge spanning the swollen waters of the Inn, sat Statio Boiodurensis.
A heavy oak turnpike barrier, banded in iron, barred the approach to the bridge. Flanking it stood the stone guard hut, its small chimney leaking peat smoke into the damp air, and behind it rose the tiled roof of the tabularium.
A toll-guard stepped out of the sentry box, his wool cloak pulled high around his neck, raising a spear across the road.
"Halt the file," the guard said, barely glancing at the column. "State your business."
Vindex didn't check his stallion until the beast’s nose was three inches from the guard’s chest.
"I am Strator Consularis Vindex, seconding remounts for the Rhine legions under direct mandate of the Governor of Noricum," Vindex snapped. "Raise the beam."
The guard didn't flinch. He had seen angry officers before. "The beam stays down until the papers clear the desk. Thirty horses can't sit on the road. Take them down to the lower pen."
"These are imperial mounts, fool," Vindex snarled. "They don't sit in the mud while civilian clerks scrape wax."
"They sit where the regulations say they sit," the guard replied, pointing with his spear butt toward the timber slipway leading down to the river terrace. "Or they don't cross the bridge. Take it up with the vilicus."
Vindex held the man’s gaze, his jaw tightening until the leather straps of his helmet bit into his cheeks. Behind him, the lead stallions shifted, their hooves clattering on the cobbles.
"Sextus," Vindex barked over his shoulder. "Take the strings down to the water. Get them tied and watered."
The drovers turned the lead lines, guiding the six strings of horses down the muddy track toward the river corral.
The enclosure sat on the gravel terrace fifteen paces below the road, right against the churning, grey current of the Inn. It was built of heavy post-and-rail oak, its far side running directly along the drop to the water, where a long timber watering trough was fed by a mountain runoff leat.
The four drovers led the strings inside, their boots sinking ankle-deep in freezing mud. They hitched the lead horses to the heavy river-facing rail above the troughs, spacing the strings so the stallions had room to lower their heads into the water without snapping at each other.
Thirty horses, heads down, immediately formed an unbroken wall of dark croups and swishing tails, completely screening the far fence from the road above.
Up on the gravel apron, the senior drover, a veteran named Marcus, swung down from his saddle. He unbuckled his cloak, looked down at the pen, then turned toward the junior recruits, Vibius and Sextus.
"You two stay with the lines," Marcus said. "Watch the troughs. If a stallion tangles his lead or starts biting, sort it out."
"It's freezing down there, Marcus," Vibius complained, wiping slush from his cheek. "Can't we rotate?"
"You'll rotate when I tell you to rotate, recruit," Marcus said, not breaking stride as he walked toward the warm sentry hut. "You're at the bottom of the roster, and that's where you stay. Keep your eyes on the beasts."
Marcus and the other senior drover slipped through the leather curtain of the guardhouse, joining the two outriders and the turnpike sentry by the glowing charcoal brazier. The heavy curtain fell shut behind them, cutting off the draft.
Vibius cursed under his breath, took his shovel from the pack-mule, and trudged down into the pen beside Sextus.
⁂
Up at the customs house, Vindex pushed through the double oak doors of the tabularium, his boots leaving greasy smears of clay across the flagstones. Behind him trotted Hilarus, the legionary librarius, clutching the boiled-leather document case against his ribs like a shield.
The office was stiflingly warm and reeked of lamp oil, damp wool, and scorched peat.
Behind a heavy oak counter sat Faustinianus, the station vilicus, a portly imperial freedman wrapped in sheepskin robes, warming his fat hands over an iron charcoal pan. Beside him sat Florianus, the counter-scribe, a thin, sour-faced man with ink-stained fingers and a pile of pine wax tablets stacked neatly beside his elbow.
Neither man looked up immediately. Florianus continued drawing the point of his iron stylus down the wax of a day-book, muttering tally numbers under his breath.
Vindex stepped to the counter and brought his fist down on the oak with a hollow crack.
"I have thirty cavalry stallions bound for the Rhine front," Vindex said. "I want the clearance stamped and the turnpike raised. Now."
Faustinianus finally looked up. His eyes were small, watery, and completely unimpressed.
"Thirty head," Faustinianus said slowly. "Entering the jurisdiction of Raetia from the province of Noricum. That is subject to the portorium Illyrici."
"These are military remounts," Vindex snapped. "Immune from tax under imperial decree."
"Nothing is immune until the records prove otherwise, officer," Faustinianus replied smoothly. "The Emperor himself doesn't cross this river without an entry in my day-book. Show the diploma."
Vindex gestured impatiently, and Hilarus scrambled forwards, fumbling with the bronze buckles of the leather tube. He extracted two tightly rolled sheets of papyrus and a three-leaf wax diptych, laying them on the counter.
Florianus picked up the first papyrus. He held it close to the oil lamp, examining the red wax seal at the bottom.
"Seal of the Legatus Augusti pro praetore of Noricum," Florianus murmured, his voice dry as parchment. "Intact. Travel warrant for thirty remounts, four drovers, two outriders, one clerk, one officer. Destination: Moguntiacum."
"You see?" Vindex said. "Now stamp the receipt, and let us be on our way."
"Not so fast," Florianus said, picking up the wax diptych. "This is the notae equorum. The descriptive manifest."
He opened the leaves, squinting at the scratches.
"Number one: bay stallion, six years, star on forehead, off-hind white. Number two: black stallion, five years, branded Pegasus on near shoulder... Thirty entries." Florianus set the diptych down, reached for a blank wax tablet, and picked up his stylus. "I will need to inspect every animal."
Vindex stepped forwards, his scale armor clinking. "Inspect them? Are you mad? That's thirty horses! It will take an hour!"
"It will take as long as it takes to ensure no unmanifested stock is crossing this border," Faustinianus said, his voice flat and unyielding. "You seem very keen to move them through unchecked. Your hostile manner is enough to raise suspicion. You can be thankful I'm not one to be suspicious, only meticulous, because if one horse has a blaze instead of a star, or if a local trader slipped a civilian mount into your string to dodge the toll, the penalty falls on my ledger. Florianus will examine the teeth, the markings, and the brands."
"The herd is certified by the legionary medicus at Lauriacum!" Vindex shouted.
"The medicus at Lauriacum doesn't answer for the publicum portorium," Florianus said. "I do. The law is explicit, officer. No verification, no stamp. No stamp, no bridge."
Vindex glared at the two bureaucrats, his hand resting on the pommel of his spatha, his chest heaving with silent fury. He knew the law. He could cut both men down where they sat, and by nightfall a cohort from the fort would have him in chains for treason against the imperial treasury.
"Do it quickly," Vindex hissed.
"I do it thoroughly, officer," Florianus said. "The Empire runs on precision, not speed."
Faustinianus held up a hand. "Before we inspect the animals, we must ensure all the documents are in order. Are you travelling with any pack-mules?"

4 — Girls from Home
Down in the mud of the pen, Vibius leaned his shovel against the outer fence and blew into his hands.
His boots were soaked through with freezing slush. Ten paces away, thirty Norican stallions stood at the troughs, their heads down, their tails twitching against the wind. Up on the road, the customs house stood shut, its windows dark behind the oiled parchment panes.
"How long will they take in there?" Sextus asked, shivering beside him.
"Hours," Vibius said. "Clerks don't care who freezes."
He turned his back on the horses, resting his arms on the top rail of the road fence, staring into the mist hanging over the track. It was thick enough to obscure the timber bridge thirty paces out, deadening the sound of the Inn to a low rumble.
"Look at that," Sextus said, nudging him. "On the road."
Coming down from the bluff were three figures, walking along the frozen ruts.
As they drew near, Vibius straightened.
They were not locals. They did not wear the coarse homespun of the Celtic river-folk or the sheepskins of the hill tribes. They had dark hair, olive skin, and Mediterranean features.
On the left walked an Iberian girl, the tallest of the three, sun-bronzed, lean, and athletic. Beside her was a Greek girl of middling height, with high cheekbones, dark ringlets escaping her hood, and an aquiline nose. On the right walked a Roman girl, slightly smaller than the other two, wrapped in an Italian wool mantle, keeping her eyes down.
Sextus threw his shoulders back. Vibius scraped the mud from his boots against the lower rail, hooked his thumbs into his sword-belt, and stood his ground.
"Well now," Sextus said, resting a boot on the bottom rail. "What brings three women down to the river in this cold?"
The Greek girl stopped at the fence, tilting her head. She looked Sextus up and down, taking in his red nose and the mud on his tunic.
"We heard thirty soldiers had arrived from the east," she said in clean, smooth Latin. "We found two boys with a shovel instead."
Sextus's face tightened, but he laughed and leaned over the wood. "We rode forty miles through the snow from Lauriacum. We're the cavalry escort. Direct orders from the Governor."
"A cavalry escort," the Iberian girl said. Her voice was lower, dry and unhurried. She walked up to the fence, resting her forearms on the top rail, looking straight at Vibius. "You have no mounts. You are minding horses for men who went inside."
"We're letting them drink," Vibius said, trying to hold her gaze. "Imperial remounts. The best war-stallions in Noricum, heading for the Rhine. They don't hand them to just anyone."
"Of course," she said. "And they left you here in the mud while they sit by the brazier."
"We're on the roster," Vibius said. "Security. Can't leave imperial stock unwatched."
He glanced past her to the Roman girl. She stood half a pace back, her face shadowed by her hood. When she felt him watching, she looked up, meeting his eyes for a second before looking down again.
"Minding horses at a trough must be very difficult," the Greek girl said. "Do they kick?"
"They're trained for the battle line," Sextus said. "What are your names? Are you off the barges?"
"We go where there is a fire," the Greek girl said.
Vibius leaned his elbows on the rail, looking between the Iberian and the Roman girl. "My shift ends when the clerks finish their books. I'm Vibius. Tenth Cohort, auxiliary cavalry. My family has land near Aquileia. If you need a place out of the wind, there is a tavern across the bridge—"
"Are those horses supposed to be down there?"
The Iberian girl spoke without inflection, cutting him off.
Vibius stopped. "What?"
She pointed past his shoulder toward the river.
Vibius and Sextus turned around.
The center span of the oak river rail was gone, drifting out into the grey current of the Inn.
Below the gap, three stallions from the middle string stood knee-deep in the water, their hooves slipping on the stones. Behind them, the second string pushed forwards, sliding in the clay. A black stallion fell down the bank, hit the water on its side, and scrambled to its feet against the current. Two more lunged after it, their lead lines catching the remaining post until the wood snapped.
"The horses!" Sextus yelled. "Vibius! The horses are in the river!"
Vibius spun back to the road fence. "Why didn't you—"
The road was empty.
There was only the yellow mud, the grey grass in the ditch, and the mist. No sound of boots, and no movement.
"Vibius!" Sextus shouted, running down the mud toward the water. "Catch the leads!"
Behind them, the leather curtain of the sentry hut opened.
Marcus, the senior drover, came out with bread in his hand. The other two drovers and the turnpike sentry followed, their swords knocking against the door frame.
"What is that shouting?" Marcus yelled.
Then he saw the river.
Ten horses were in the water, fighting the current, while ten more crowded the gap in the broken fence, tangled in their ropes and rearing in the mud.
Marcus dropped his bread into the dirt. His face went dark red.
"You fools!" Marcus roared. "The herd is out!"
He ran down the slipway, sliding on the wet clay. The other drovers ran after him, grabbing ropes and halters from the rails.
Up at the customs house, the double doors flew open and slammed against the stone wall.
Vindex came through the doorway, his scale armour clattering, his sword half-drawn. Hilarus followed, clutching his document tubes against his chest. Behind them came Faustinianus, the station master, shouting about the leat and the road terrace, while Florianus, the scribe, hurried after him with his wax tablet under his arm.
All four men ran to the edge of the terrace to see the damage.
"My horses!" Vindex shouted, seeing the herd in the water. "Get into the river! Cut them off from the current!"
He ran down the slipway, his boots sliding through the mud, with Hilarus and the two customs men scrambling after him to the gravel bank.
Men were shouting, swearing, and splashing through the grey water. Horses were squealing, rearing, and lunging against the current, their heavy hooves throwing freezing spray over everyone. Marcus had a big bay by the cheek-strap, roaring at Vibius to get around the flank with a rope, while Vibius stumbled through the mud, half-blind with freezing water and tears of sheer panic.

5 — Ash and Wax
From the birch scrub behind the stone tabularium, Tiata and Taran watched the post uncoil.
The crash came from the river pen—the sharp snap of pine pegs giving way under the weight of thirty horses, followed by the heavy splash of the center oak rail dropping into the shallows.
Tigratavā and Tove had timed their work to the breath. Down at the river fence, out of sight of the road, Tove’s weight had cleared the loosened timbers, while Tigratavā’s knife sliced through the remaining hemp lead-hitches with three quick strokes. A dozen stallions surged through the breach, their iron shoes clattering on the wet gravel before plunging into the freezing grey current of the Inn.
Above on the road, Thana, Toria, and Tacita were already moving. The moment Toria pointed toward the water, the three women dropped below the road berm, slipping into the willow gully to begin their climb back to the ridge. Tigratavā and Tove melted into the river mist a moment later, leaving thirty panicked horses and two terrified recruits in the mud.
Then the double doors of the customs house flew wide.
Vindex burst onto the gravel terrace, his sword drawn, shouting orders that were drowned out by the roar of the water. Behind him came Hilarus clutching his leather document tubes, followed by Faustinianus and Florianus, their sheepskins flapping as they scrambled down the muddy slipway after the drovers.
Every man at the post was running for the river.
Tiata touched Taran’s arm. "Now."
They crossed the gravel without hurrying, their boots making no sound on the wet stones.
The double doors stood wide open, the flagstones inside smeared with yellow mud from the boots of the men who had just fled.
The room was warm, smelling of scorched peat, olive oil, and damp wool.
On the oak counter lay the administrative life of the frontier.
The two papyrus scrolls bearing the red wax seal of the Governor of Noricum sat beside the open diptych. The wax of the notae equorum showed sixty lines of scratched Latin—names, ages, brands, and markings for thirty imperial stallions. Beside it sat the open day-book of the portorium Illyrici, its pine leaves spread flat, covered in columns of tax tallies and ledger numbers.
Taran stepped to the counter. For three years at Lorch, she had watched the Praefectus curse these very tablets—the duplicate rolls, the fear of missing entries, the panic whenever an auditor rode in from the south.
"This is what holds them together," Taran said quietly. "Wax and ink."
In the center of the flagstones sat the three-legged iron brazier, its peat coals glowing red. Along the back wall, pine shelving held years of customs rolls, stacked in bundles of dry papyrus tied with flax cord. On the floor sat two large earthenware jars of lamp oil, plugged with rags.
Taran pulled the rag from the first jar and tipped the vessel across the counter. The dark olive oil washed across the diptych, the travel warrants, and the day-book, pooling in the grooves of the oak.
Tiata took the second jar, walked the length of the shelving, and emptied it down the papyrus bundles and pine uprights.
She stepped back to the brazier, hooked her boot beneath the iron rim, and kicked the bowl sideways.
The glowing coals spilled across the flagstones, rolling straight into the pool beneath the counter.
The oil caught with a dull, hollow thud.
A sheet of yellow flame climbed the counter legs, consuming the governor’s papyrus warrants in seconds. The beeswax of the diptych bubbled, melted, and dissolved into black slag. Across the back wall, the oil-soaked archives caught fire, thick black smoke rolling up against the timber ceiling and spreading toward the doorway.
They stepped onto the terrace. Tiata caught the bronze ring of the right-hand door and pulled it shut, leaving the left open a hand’s breadth to draw the draft.
They crossed the road at a steady walk, entered the willow ravine, and climbed the rock shelves into the granite gorge.
Thilda stood waiting at the timber line, holding the leads of the mounts. Thana, Toria, and Tacita were already in their saddles, their cloaks drawn tight against the damp cold. Tigratavā and Tove rode up through the hazel a moment later, their leggings soaked with river spray, their faces flushed from the climb.
Tiata and Taran swung onto their horses.
Through the bare birch branches, the slate roof of the tabularium sat eighty paces below them.
A thin wisp of grey smoke began to leak from beneath the eaves. It thickened, darkening to soot, and then a jagged tongue of orange flame cracked through the mortar of the chimney.

6 — Fire
Marcus hauled the big bay onto the gravel bar, cold water pouring from the horse's withers in grey sheets.
The stallion lunged, iron shoes slipping on the wet river stones, before Marcus got two turns of the lead rope around his forearm and threw his weight against the bit. Beside him, Sextus threw himself into the shallows, grabbing the trailing halter of a second mount and dragging its head toward the spit.
"Get the lines!" Marcus roared over the rushing current. "Vibius, you fool, get behind the black!"
Vibius was waist-deep in the grey current of the Inn. The snowmelt was numbing his legs to iron, the cold biting through his wool trousers so fast his knees refused to bend. A loose horse brushed past him, its shoulder knocking him sideways into the current, and he came up spitting freezing sand, his shovel lost downstream.
Up on the gravel terrace, Faustinianus stood clutching the skirts of his sheepskin robe above the mud, his plump face pale.
"The leat!" the station master shouted down from the bank. "They will trample the stone leat! Those beasts are imperial property under customs jurisdiction!"
Vindex did not look up. He had his left arm locked over the neck of an unruly three-year-old, his scale armour dragging him down in the mud, his sword banging against his thigh. He hauled the horse out of the current onto the gravel, shouting at the two outriders to form a cordon with their ropes.
The wind shifted, drawing down from the granite bluffs behind the road.
It brought a smell that did not belong to the river.
Florianus, the scribe, was the first to stop. He stood on the lower slipway with his wax tablet tucked under his arm, his ink-stained fingers frozen stiff. He lifted his chin, sniffing the air, then turned his head toward the road terrace.
His mouth opened, but no sound came out. He raised one arm and pointed.
Hilarus, standing five paces below him in the mud with the leather document tubes, followed the gesture.
"Sir," Hilarus croaked.
Vindex wiped freezing river water from his eyebrows with the back of his forearm. "Shut your mouth and take this lead—"
"Sir! The station!"
Vindex turned.
Above them, thick black smoke was pouring from the stone eaves of the tabularium. It did not come in a thin draft from the chimney, but in heavy, rolling clouds that pressed out from beneath the grey roof slates. Through the rectangular window frames, the oiled parchment panes were already gone, replaced by a dull red glare.
Faustinianus let out a shriek that cracked on the wind.
"The books!" the station master screamed, dropping his robes into the mud. "The rolls! The registers!"
He scrambled up the frozen slipway on his hands and knees, town sandals sliding in the clay, clawing at the gravel with his bare fingers. Florianus ran past him, taking the slope in desperate, lunging strides.
Every man on the riverbank froze, heads turning toward the road terrace.
In that second of shock, the third string tore loose.
Five stallions, tied head-to-tail on a single braided hemp line, took fright at the sudden roar from the terrace. The lead horse shied violently from the smoke, wheeled into the deep water, and lost its footing on the gravel shelf. The current took its legs out from under it.
The heavy beast went over on its side with a massive plunge, dragging the second horse off the ledge by the coupling rope.
"The line!" Marcus bellowed, dropping his bay's headstall. "Catch the third string!"
It was already too late.
The weight of two half-ton horses tumbling into the main channel jerked the remaining three off the bank. They hit the deep water in a thrashing tangle of ropes and limbs. The lead line twisted around their hocks, binding their legs together. Heads broke the surface for two heartbeats—nostrils flared wide, ears pinned flat, rolling white eyes—before the heavy current of the Inn sucked them under.
The five linked stallions swept past the timber bridge pilings in a wash of grey foam, tumbling downstream toward the gorge of the Danube, gone in twenty paces.
Marcus stood frozen in the shallows, the empty lead rope slipping from his numb fingers.
"Leave them!" Vindex roared, his face white with rage. "Get to the post! Get to the post!"
Vindex ran for the slipway, water sloshing in his boots, his scale cuirass heavy as lead.
By the time he crested the road terrace, Florianus was already stumbling back from the doorway, his forearms thrown up across his eyes, his hair singed.
Heat poured from the open door.
Inside, the room was a furnace. The archive shelves were a solid sheet of orange fire that roared as the draft from the open door caught the flue. On the counter, the papyrus warrants were gone, reduced to grey flakes spinning in the updraft. The wax of the diptych was bubbling into grease, running in smoking black rivulets across the flagstones.
Faustinianus reached the threshold on his knees, gasping in the thick soot.
"The day-book," the station master wheezed, tears cutting clean lines through the soot on his cheeks. "The cross-tallies... twenty years of the portorium..."
He lunged forwards, reaching both hands toward the flaming counter, but Vindex caught him by the collar of his sheepskin and hauled him backwards onto the gravel.
"You'll burn, you idiot!" Vindex snarled.
"My ledgers!" Faustinianus wailed, thrashing in Vindex's grip. "The receipts! The treasury audits! I am answerable to the governor for every copper!"
"The travel warrant!" Vindex roared, shaking the fat man until his teeth clicked. "The duplicate warrant! Where did you put it?"
"On the counter!" Faustinianus cried, pointing a trembling hand into the wall of flame. "You saw me! Florianus laid it beside the diptych!"
A heavy ceiling timber gave way inside with a loud crack, sending a shower of burning pine sparks and broken roof slates crashing down across the counter. The blaze surged, driving both men three paces back across the road.
Behind them, Marcus and the other drovers came running up from the riverbank, their breath ragged, dragging what was left of the herd by their halters.
Vibius and Sextus followed, soaked to the throat, shivering so violently their scale shirts rattled against their ribs.
Vindex turned on them, his face black with soot, his right hand gripping the pommel of his spatha.
"Who was in that room?" Vindex demanded, his voice dropping into a dangerous rasp. "Who touched the brazier?"
"Nobody, sir!" Marcus said, struggling to hold the remaining mounts on the road. "We were in the sentry box! We ran out the second the rail gave way!"
Vindex walked toward the two recruits, his boots crunching on the frozen gravel. "You two. You were standing ten paces from that door. Did someone cross this road?"
Vibius swallowed hard. His face was bloodless, his lower lip split from a horse's knee, his eyes wide with terror.
"There were... there were women, sir," Vibius stammered.
The silence that followed was broken only by the roar of the fire inside the stone walls and the cracking of timber.
Vindex stopped two paces away. "Women."
"Three of them, sir," Sextus said, his voice rising in panic. "Mediterranean women. Greek and Roman. They came down from the bluff, walking along the ruts. They stopped at the fence to speak to us, and then—"
Marcus stepped forwards and struck Vibius across the face with his open palm.
The blow knocked the recruit sideways into the ditch mud.
"Incompetent cur!" Marcus bellowed. "You let five imperial stallions drown in the river because you were asleep on your feet, and now you invent three Roman women out of the snow!"
"They were there!" Vibius sobbed from the mud, clutching his cheek. "I swear it by Mars! They were standing at the rail! The dark one pointed at the water—she asked if the horses were supposed to be down there!"
"Silence!" Vindex drew his spatha six inches from the scabbard, the iron clicking against the bronze throat. He held the blade flat against the cold air, looking down at the shivering boy. "Three Roman women. Walking alone on the Norican frontier in the dead of winter, stopping to chat with two drovers holding shovels."
He slammed the blade back into the sheath with a hard thud.
"If you speak that lie again," Vindex said quietly, "I will hang you from the bridge timbers myself."
Faustinianus was still sitting in the slush, his hands buried in his grey hair, staring at the collapsed roof. "The auditors will be here when the snow clears. There are no duplicates. The Noricum registers are ash. The customs receipts for three districts... gone."
"You think you are ruined?" Vindex barked a short, bitter laugh.
He walked to the edge of the terrace and pointed down at the riverbank below.
On the gravel bar, twenty-five shivering stallions stood in the mud, their coats crusted with ice, uncounted, unexamined, and unrecorded.
"Look down there, clerk," Vindex said, his voice flat with cold fury. "Twenty-five horses out of thirty. Five drowned in the gorge. No travel warrant. No imperial seal. No descriptive manifest. No customs clearance. To every auxiliary commander between this river and the Rhine, I am an officer driving stolen stock."

7 — The Limesgruppe
The turf-and-stone rampart of Kastell Boiodurum sat two hundred paces back from the river, its timber north gate looking down over the wharves.
Through the falling mist, the column filed up the gravel track from the bridgehead. Marcus and the two outriders led the twenty-five surviving stallions, the beasts shivering violently, ice crusted thick along their withers and flanks. Behind them stumbled Vibius and Sextus, eyes fixed on the slush, their soaked tunics stiffening in the wind.
At the tail of the file walked Vindex, with Faustinianus waddling beside him, screaming into the empty air.
The timber gate stood half-barred. A dozen auxiliary infantrymen of the Second Breucian Cohort stood on the catwalk above the ditch, their oval shields resting against the parapet, watching the black column of smoke rising from the customs house across the road.
Prefect Trebonius was already in the gateway.
He had held command of the Norican border post for six years. He was fifty years old, thick-set, with grey hair cut close to the scalp and an old scar running through his left ear. He stood with his thumbs hooked into his heavy officer’s belt, his red wool cloak pinned against the damp chill, watching the greasy soot settle across his gravel apron.
Faustinianus shoved past the gate guards, clawing at Trebonius’s forearm with soot-blackened hands.
"Arrest him!" the station master shrieked. "Treason against the treasury! He burned the station! Twenty years of imperial registers! The day-books, the tallies, the provincial seals—all of it ash on the hearth!"
Trebonius pulled his arm free with deliberate slowness. "Faustinianus. You are dripping river mud onto my boots."
"The portorium is destroyed!" Faustinianus wailed, pointing a shaking finger back toward the river. "He refused the inspection! He threatened my scribe! He overturned the charcoal brazier to destroy his own fraud!"
Vindex stepped into the gate passage, his scale armor clattering. "I am an officer of the provincial staff, Prefect. I am seconding remounts under direct mandate of the Governor of Noricum. I have twenty-five prime cavalry stallions standing in the wind. Open your stables and issue forage."
Trebonius looked past Vindex’s shoulder at the horses standing in the ditch. He counted the files with an experienced eye.
"Twenty-five," Trebonius said, his voice flat.
"Twenty-five," Vindex repeated.
"The transit manifest from Lauriacum reached my desk three days ago," Trebonius said. "Carried by the imperial post. Thirty Norican war-stallions, sound of wind and limb, destined for the Rhine legions. Where are the other five?"
Vindex’s jaw set hard. "The river fence gave way. The snowmelt took them."
"Five imperial mounts drowned under a bridge," Trebonius said, "thirty paces from a customs station that burned to the foundation while you stood in the road."
"It was an accident," Vindex snapped. "The timber was rotten. I need stabling while my clerk draws up duplicate warrants."
"You need stabling," Trebonius said. He stepped out through the wicket onto the ditch apron, his boots crunching in the slush. "With what authority, officer? Show the customs clearance."
"The clearance was on the counter when the room caught fire," Vindex hissed. "You can smell the burning parchment from here!"
"Then you have no clearance," Trebonius said. "The law along this river is very simple. Noricum ends at the water. Raetia begins across the timber. Nothing crosses that bridge without the stamp of the portorium Illyrici. No soldier enters my gates with uncertified stock."
"These are not civilian pack-mules!" Vindex roared, his hand dropping to the pommel of his spatha. "These are remounts for the Limes Germanicus! The legions are massing on the Rhine, and the Emperor is already at Moguntiacum waiting for stock! If I am delayed on this river by a stubborn garrison commander, your name goes to the governor before sunset!"
Trebonius did not move. He looked down at Vindex’s hand resting on the hilt of his sword, then lifted his gaze to Vindex’s eyes.
"The Emperor is at Moguntiacum because the frontier is unravelling," Trebonius said quietly. "The Danube couriers talk. The riverboat men from Castra Regina talk louder. Fort Lorch burned to the ground and its garrison fled to the Rhine. Fort Dalkingen collapsed into rubble. The heavy horse at Aalen spent the winter behind their stone walls because someone was houghing cavalry mounts in the defiles."
Trebonius pointed a blunt finger toward the smoking ruins across the road.
"Now thirty miles from my post, an imperial customs house goes up in flame, five war-stallions drown in the current, and you arrive at my ditch demanding stabling without a scrap of wax to your name."
"You think the Alemanni did this?" Vindex gave a short, bitter laugh. "Here? In Noricum?"
"The river merchants don't call them Alemanni," Trebonius said. "They call them the Limesgruppe. Fifty renegades. Deserters from the auxiliary wings who know our patrol schedules, who shoot heavy composite bows and dismantle outposts without leaving a footprint."
He walked past Vindex toward the head of the horse file, stopping in front of Marcus.
"How did that river rail break, drover?" Trebonius asked.
Marcus swallowed, glancing back at the two recruits standing in the slush. He reached out, caught Vibius by the scruff of his soaked tunic, and dragged him forwards into the dirt before the prefect.
"Tell him," Marcus snarled. "Tell him the fairy tale you told the Strator."
Vibius fell to his knees in the mud. His teeth were chattering so hard he could barely shape his words.
"There were... there were women, sir," Vibius stammered.
Trebonius stared down at the boy. "Women."
"Three of them, sir," Vibius said, shivering violently. "They came along the road from the bluff. They stood at the fence. They were Mediterranean—proper women, not locals. A dark Iberian, a Greek with an aquiline nose, and a Roman girl in a wool mantle."
The gate guards on the rampart shifted, a murmur running along the parapet.
Trebonius’s face darkened. "Women. You lost five war-stallions in the current because you were talking to three girls."
"The Iberian pointed at the water!" Vibius cried out, desperate now. "She asked if the horses were supposed to be in the river! When we turned around, the fence was gone! When we looked back, the road was empty! They vanished, sir! They melted into the mist!"
"Silence," Trebonius said.
The word was quiet, but it stopped the boy's voice instantly.
Trebonius turned to Vindex. "The river taverns are full of this garbage. Down at the wharves, the bargees drink cheap wine and swear a giantess with a scythe cut down four auxiliaries at Aalen. They swear a banshee with blue war-paint drops out of the trees outside Buch to rip men’s throats open. Now your drovers stand before my gate and tell me three Roman girls walked through knee-deep drifts on the Norican frontier to sabotage an oak fence."
He looked at Vibius with pure contempt.
"If the Limesgruppe even exists, it is at least fifty hardened horsemen cutting our cavalry to pieces in the west," Trebonius said. "If there's any truth in the fable, they are Roman deserters who know our doctrine. And you bring me two cowardly recruits who invent women out of the fog to hide their own incompetence."
"They will face the vine-staff," Vindex said between clenched teeth. "I will have them flogged till the skin peels."
"No!" Faustinianus shoved himself between the two officers, trembling with outrage. "Under the authority of the Fiscus, those animals are evidence of an ongoing fiscal inquiry! No uncertified horse enters military stabling until the procurator at Ovilava sends an auditor!"
Trebonius looked down at the fat clerk, then past him at the twenty-five horses shivering in the freezing wind, their heads hanging low.
"Quiet, Faustinianus," Trebonius said. "You have no station, no records, and no roof over your head. I am not leaving twenty-five prime cavalry stallions to founder in the slush outside my gate because a customs clerk lost his ink pots."
He turned toward the gatehouse and raised his voice to the duty centurion.
"Form a detail," Trebonius ordered. "Bring the herd through to the secondary lines behind the granary. Put them on dry straw, rub the ice from their withers, and issue half-rations of barley. Post four Breucian sentries on the stable doors. Nobody moves those beasts without my written mark."
Vindex let out a tight breath, stepping forwards into the passage. "My drovers will see to the grooming—"
"Your drovers will see to the outer barracks," Trebonius said, stepping across his path. "The horses come inside. You and your men remain billeted under guard. You have no transit clearance, Strator. You have no descriptive manifest, and you are five mounts short of your Lauriacum tally. Until a courier rides from Ovilava with fresh warrants, not a single hoof crosses that timber bridge into Raetia."
Vindex held Trebonius’s gaze, but the older officer did not yield an inch.
"Centurion," Trebonius called back into the yard. "Take the recruits. Put them on stable detail under the guard corporal. If either of them speaks of women again, give them ten strokes with the vine-staff to clear their heads."
The heavy oak gates creaked open.
The drovers led the twenty-five stallions through into the courtyard, their iron shoes clattering on the cobbles, leaving Faustinianus standing in the ditch, clutching his mud-stained robes in furious, helpless silence.

8 — The Cost of Truth
From the shelf of granite two hundred feet above the gorge, the view of the valley floor was unobstructed.
The black plume from the customs house had thinned to a greasy smudge that drifted north across the swollen Danube. Down on the river terrace, the slate roof of the tabularium had fallen in, leaving four charred stone walls enclosing a pit of smoking charcoal.
Below, at Kastell Boiodurum, the courtyard was a hive of movement.
Through the bare branches of a stunted pine, Tigratavā watched the north gate through narrowed eyes. "They are taking them into the secondary lines," she said, her voice low. "Behind the granaries."
Beside her on the ledge, Toria sat with a broad grin on her face. "Look at the fat clerk. He's still waving his arms in the mud."
Down on the road, Faustinianus was being guided toward the timber sentry hut by two auxiliary infantrymen, his sheepskin mantle caked in yellow clay. At the fort gate, the heavy oak portals had swung open. Marcus and the two outriders led the twenty-five surviving Norican stallions through the gap, their wet coats dark against the slush. Behind them walked Vibius and Sextus, marched between four guards with drawn spathas.
Vindex stood alone on the ditch apron for a moment before turning on his heel and following the column inside, flanked by the duty centurion.
The heavy timbers closed behind them, and the iron drop-bar thudded home into its brackets.
"The gate is shut," Toria said, looking over her shoulder at the others. "The horses are in the stalls, and the drovers are under guard."
"They will stay under guard," Taran said from where she sat against the rock face. "That recruit told the truth, and his officer struck him in the mouth for it."
"He looked so earnest," Toria said. "Standing there with his thumbs in his belt, telling me about his family farm near Aquileia. Vibius, he said his name was. Then I asked him if the stallions were supposed to be in the river, and he looked ready to weep."
"The bridge is closed," Tiata noted, stepping back from the rim. "The commander down there won't let those horses cross into Raetia without fresh wax from his superiors. And the fat clerk has no desk left to write it."
"How long will that take?" Toria asked.
"Weeks," Taran said. "At Lorch, whenever a shipment arrived without a manifest, the Praefectus had to send a courier to the provincial governor at Moguntiacum. The clerk drafts a query. The courier rides two hundred miles. The staff office checks the duplicate rolls, finds the clerk who signed the original, stamps a provisional warrant in triplicate, and sends the rider back. If the weather is bad, the horses sit in the mud until the forage runs out."
"And the Emperor is on the Rhine," Thilda added. "Waiting for fresh cavalry mounts to face the Alemanni."
"He will wait a long time," Thana said. "Twenty-five horses eating fort barley while three offices argue over who pays for five drowned stallions and a burned customs house."
"We have no reason to linger," Tove said. "Rome can choke on its own soot."
"Our road lies east," Tigratavā said. "Dacia is still a long ride down the Danube."

9 — The Ledger of the Empire
The fortress of Lauriacum sat on the high gravel terrace above the Danube, its grey ashlar walls guarding the great military highway of Noricum.
Snowmelt dripped from the eaves of the legionary principia into the stone drainage gutters. Inside the heated record hall behind the basilica, charcoal braziers kept the damp chill from the archive racks where the rolls of Legio II Italica filled cedar pigeonholes from floor to ceiling.
Tribune Caeso Licinius sat behind a heavy oak table, reviewing the monthly forage returns from the Enns valley. Opposite him sat Aurelius Hermias, a lean imperial freedman sent from the procurator’s office at Ovilava to audit the military transport manifests.
A leather curtain parted, and the camp cornicularius entered, followed by a courier spattered to the chest in yellow mud.
The courier unbuckled his bronze cylinder and laid it on the cedar boards.
"From Kastell Boiodurum," the courier said, his voice hoarse from forty miles against the river wind. "Prefect Trebonius dispatched me at dawn."
Licinius broke the clay seal with his thumb. He unrolled the papyrus, smoothing the curling edges against the wood, and began to read.
Hermias watched him over the rim of an unrolled parchment tally. "Has Vindex cleared the crossing?"
Licinius did not answer immediately. His eyes moved down the lines of Trebonius’s neat administrative Latin. His brow furrowed, his jaw tightened, and the paper began to tremble slightly in his grip.
"He has not cleared the crossing," Licinius said, his voice dangerously level.
Hermias set down his reed pen. "Has the ice held him up?"
"The customs post is gone," Licinius said. He read directly from the sheet. "Statio Boiodurensis destroyed by fire on the fourth day before the Kalends of March. The tabularium burned to the foundations. All tax registers, day-books, and archive rolls of the portorium Illyrici destroyed."
Hermias rose from his stool so fast his inkpot rocked on the stand. "The portorium registers? Twenty years of provincial tolls?"
"Ash," Licinius said, looking up. "Faustinianus has accused Vindex of setting the brazier to his desk to cover fraudulent stock. Vindex claims the station master was asleep when the chimney caught."
"Where are the thirty horses?" Hermias demanded.
Licinius turned the sheet over. "Twenty-five horses are impounded in the auxiliary stables at Boiodurum. Trebonius has refused them transit into Raetia."
"And the other five?"
"Drowned," Licinius said flatly. "In the Inn. Under the bridge. The river fence collapsed while the beasts were at the trough."
Hermias stared at the tribune in disbelief. "Five Norican war-stallions drowned in sight of a Roman fort? How?"
Licinius read further, his mouth twisting into an expression of bitter disgust. "Trebonius questioned the drovers. The two recruits at the paddock claim they were approached at the road fence by three Mediterranean women. An Iberian, a Greek, and a Roman."
The room went completely silent. Even the cornicularius by the door stopped turning his roster.
"Women," Hermias said softly.
"Women," Licinius repeated, tossing the papyrus onto the table. "Three civilised women walking through winter mud forty miles from the nearest town. One engaged them in conversation, pointed at the water, and asked if the stallions were supposed to be in the river. When the recruits looked back, the fence was broken and the women were gone."
Hermias let out a dry, rasping laugh that carried no humor. "A fairy tale. The drovers fell asleep over their shovels, the stallions broke the timber, and the boys invented three phantoms to save their skin from the vine-staff."
"And Vindex lost his head," Licinius said, leaning forwards. "The Emperor is sitting at Moguntiacum with the Rhine army, waiting for remounts to break the Alemanni. The entire northern command is crying out for heavy horse, and twenty-five prime Norican stallions are locked in an auxiliary ditch because a civilian clerk and a staff officer cannot settle an argument over a burned table."
He stood up, pushing his stool back against the stone wall.
"This ends today," Licinius said. "The governor will not answer to the Emperor for five drowned horses and a closed frontier."
He turned to the cornicularius. "Summon Centurion Domitius from the cavalry lines. Immediately."
The clerk saluted and hurried through the leather curtain.
Licinius walked to the wall map of the Danube provinces. His finger traced the road west from Lauriacum, following the river past Ovilava to the confluence at Boiodurum.
"The horses will not stay in that ditch," Licinius said. "Domitius will take twenty veteran troopers from the legionary cavalry. They will ride light, take custody of the twenty-five stallions directly from Trebonius, and cross the Inn. I will draft a provisional military transit warrant under the governor's direct seal. The horses will be driven across Raetia without setting foot in another customs house."
"And the destroyed station?" Hermias asked, his voice sharp. "Who answers to the procurator for twenty years of lost revenue?"
"You will ride with Domitius," Licinius replied, turning from the map. "You will take four legionaries to secure the prisoners. Vindex is relieved of duty and placed under formal military arrest. His drovers will be put in irons and marched back to Lauriacum to face tribunal for dereliction and gross negligence. Faustinianus and his scribe will be delivered in a cart to the procurator’s court at Ovilava. If Faustinianus burned his own ledgers to hide a shortfall, the inquisitors will have the truth out of him before the week ends."
"And Trebonius?" Hermias asked. "He barred the crossing."
"Trebonius acted within the letter of the law," Licinius said coldly. "Which is the only reason he is not being marched back in irons beside them. But he will receive an official reprimand for allowing an imperial customs station to burn under the eyes of his garrison without raising a bucket."
Half an hour later, the courtyard of the principia rang with the clatter of iron shoes on wet cobbles.
Centurion Domitius stood before the steps, his scale cuirass gleaming beneath a heavy wool travelling cloak, twenty veteran troopers of Legio II Italica mounted in two disciplined ranks behind him. Behind them stood four pack-mules and a covered supply wagon fitted with iron window bars.
Licinius came down the stone steps, holding two sealed parchment rolls bound in green silk cord. He handed the first to Domitius.
"The provisional transit warrant," Licinius said. "Twenty-five Norican cavalry stallions, passing through Raetia under direct legionary escort. If an auxiliary officer or a customs clerk so much as looks at your reins, show him that seal and ride over him."
Domitius took the roll and slid it into his leather dispatch case. "Understood, Tribune."
Licinius handed the second roll to Hermias, who was climbing into the front bench of the wagon. "The arrest warrants. Bring Vindex and his drovers back to Lauriacum, then take the clerks to Ovilava."
Domitius raised his right arm, wheeled his black charger, and led the column toward the west gate.
The heavy iron-shod hooves hammered across the flagstones, kicking up sprays of melting slush, marching west along the military highway to clear the border.

10 — River-Gate
They tracked the southern bank of the Danube east for three days, keeping off the paved military highway whenever the ground allowed.
The Norican countryside was thick with Roman authority. Every five miles brought another stone watchtower, a courier post, or a timber stockade minding the river crossings. They moved between the limestone ridges and the river flats, bypassing the garrison at Arelape by cutting through the scrub behind the town before circling back to the water.
Ten miles past the garrison, the terrain tightened.
The granite bluffs of the Bohemian Massif pressed hard against the southern bank, squeezing the road into a rocky ledge twenty paces wide, with vertical cliffs rising on the right and the grey, swirling current of the Danube dropping off on the left.
Around the bend, blocking the defile, sat the river-gate checkpoint of Namare.
The Romans had built their post where the rock pinched narrowest. A two-storey timber watchtower anchored the riverside, joined to a stone-and-timber gatehouse built directly against the rock wall. Between the two buildings ran a heavy oak turnpike barrier, banded in iron, dropping into a slotted timber receiver on the far side.
The eight women reined in behind a shoulder of broken scree, fifty paces short of the bend, hidden in dwarf pine and hazel scrub.
Tigratavā dismounted, passed her reins to Tove, and crawled to the lip of the rock.
From her vantage point, twenty feet above the road, she had a clear line of sight through an unglazed rectangular window in the upper wall of the gatehouse.
Inside the small record room, a lone scribe was working at a long trestle table. Parchment rolls and pine wax tablets covered the boards, alongside inkpots and scrapers. At the far end of the room stood a timber framework built against the stonework, divided into dozens of square pigeonholes packed with bundles of provincial customs rolls.
In the center of the table sat a heavy three-flame brass oil lamp, its wicks burning with steady yellow points. Directly beside it sat an earthenware gallon jar, still wet around the neck where the scribe had just topped off the reservoir.
Tigratavā lowered her gaze to the road below.
An ox-cart loaded with heavy timber casks stood halted before the lowered barrier. Two auxiliary guards were engaged in an angry argument with the carter, poking spears into the load and waving a wax tablet in the merchant’s face. A third guard leaned against the watchtower railing with his thumbs in his belt, watching the dispute.
Taran crawled up through the briars and settled beside Tigratavā, pulling her yew bow from her shoulder.
Tacita moved without a word. She dropped down the loose scree behind the rock shoulder, stepping from boulder to boulder without dislodging a pebble, and melted into the dead ground behind the stone rear wall of the gatehouse.
Tigratavā notched a heavy trilobate shaft to her recurve bow, her eyes fixed on the open window. Beside her, Taran laid an iron broadhead against the riser of her yew stave, sighting down the slope toward the ox.
Down on the road, Tacita emerged from the blind corner of the masonry. She dropped low beneath the road barrier, slid her hand along the oak beam to the slotted receiver post, and worked the heavy iron locking pin free with a firm, steady twist. With the pin in hand, she backed into the shadow of the cliff.
Tigratavā drew to her ear. Taran drew in the same breath.
Tigratavā loosed first, and Taran’s bow hummed an instant later.
Tigratavā’s shaft flew through the rectangular window opening. The heavy iron head struck the earthenware oil jar dead-center.
The clay vessel shattered. A gallon of refined olive oil erupted across the trestle table, drenching the dry papyrus rolls and cascading straight over the three burning wicks.
The oil caught with a hollow roar.
A sheet of yellow flame leaped from the table, lashing the timber framework against the wall. The dry rolls of customs archives in the pigeonholes caught instantly, feeding the blaze until a wall of solid fire climbed to the roof beams. The scribe screeched, kicked his stool over, and threw himself headlong through the interior doorway into the passage.
Down on the road, Taran’s broadhead struck home.
The heavy iron point buried itself deep into the rump of the near ox.
The beast bellowed, threw its head up, and lunged forwards in blind agony.
The second ox was dragged into the charge. The team threw their combined weight and the momentum of the loaded cart straight into the unpinned barrier arm.
Without the iron pin to hold the far end, the force sheared the pivot post clean through with a loud, tearing groan. The heavy oak beam swung wildly across the road, slamming the two arguing guards into the ditch mud, before the cart surged over the broken wood and clattered through the gap.
The terrified oxen bolted down the eastern road at a dead run, hooves hammering the gravel, the timber casks bouncing and crashing in the bed. Behind them, the carter ran through the broken gate, screaming curses into the dust, while the third guard ran after him with his spear raised.
High above the river, the watchtower horn began to blare.
Auxiliaries tumbled down the timber ladders, snatching up bronze fire-buckets and shouting in confusion as they ran toward the riverbank to draw water for the roaring gatehouse roof.
Tove rode around the bend at a trot, leading the mounts of Tigratavā, Taran, and Tacita by their halter ropes.
She pulled up thirty paces short of the barrier, where Tacita stepped out from the rock face and the two archers dropped down the clay bank from the scrub.
As their boots touched the stirrups, the ground behind them began to tremble.
Tiata, Thilda, Thana, and Toria rounded the corner of the defile at a flying gallop, riding four abreast along the narrow shelf. Their horses’ iron shoes threw sparks from the granite, weapons raised, completely dominating the track.
The guards scrambling with water buckets on the riverbank turned, froze, and scattered into the rocks to avoid the charge.
Tacita, Tigratavā, and Taran swung into their saddles. Tove dug her heels into her mare’s ribs, and the four joined the rear of the file.
They swept through the broken barrier at full speed, riding through the falling soot and the heat radiating from the burning gatehouse.
No Roman had time to level a spear.
They kept their horses at a hard gallop along the river terrace, leaving Namare behind them in black smoke.
Half a mile down the road, where the track bent away from the Danube, they passed the ox-cart. It lay overturned in the ditch on its side, the wheels spinning slowly in the cold air, broken casks leaking red wine across the slush, while the winded oxen stood tangled in their harness further down the brush.
The eight women did not check their stride, sweeping past the wreckage and driving east into the open plains of Noricum.

11 — Two Gates
The hollow beneath the granite shelf sat two hundred feet above the gorge, tucked deep into the rocks where no river sentry could spot the glow.
Tove had a kettle of salt pork and dried peas bubbling over the coals. She stirred the pot with a flat piece of pine, laughing so hard her shoulders shook, while Toria stood by the rock face reenacting the carter at the turnpike.
"He had both hands in the air," Toria gasped, clutching her ribs. "Screaming at the oxen, screaming at the guards! The barrier arm was wedged right through his front wheel, dragging along the gravel, and two casks were rolling down the ditch spitting red wine over everything!"
Taran sat on her saddle-pack, wiping road grit from her bow-stave, grinning from ear to ear. "I put that broadhead right in the meat of the rump. The beast didn't even drop his head—he just took the turnpike with him."
Thana looked across the fire at Tacita, who sat on a boulder quietly cleaning her thumbnail with the tip of her knife.
"Did that receiver pin give you any trouble, Tacita?"
Tacita looked up, her dark eyes catching the firelight, her mouth twitching into a small, dry smile.
"No," Tacita said. "It was greased."
"Imperial maintenance," Thana purred, stretching her long legs toward the embers. "Everything done by regulation. They oil the iron so we don't strain our wrists."
Even Tigratavā let out an appreciative chuckle from where she sat checking the fletching on her arrows, shaking her head at the fire.
"And Boiodurum," Toria said, turning around, her face flushed with delight. "Don't forget the drover boy! Vibius! He stood at that fence puffing his chest out, telling me about his family farm near Aquileia! Offering to buy me wine across the bridge!"
"While Tove and Tigratavā were dismantling the river fence behind his back," Taran laughed.
"He was so busy trying to impress her he didn't hear thirty horses sliding into the water," Thana said, her voice dripping with amusement. "And then the sergeant knocked him flat into the mud for daring to say three women had been standing in the road."
"The poor lad told the absolute truth," Taran said, wiping a tear of laughter from her eye. "Word for word, exactly what happened. And they hit him in the mouth for it."
"A Roman officer cannot allow women to steal thirty horses," Thilda added in her dry, steady cadence. "If fifty barbarian deserters cross the river, he keeps his dignity. If three girls talk to his sentries, he loses his mind."
"And the fat clerk," Toria gloated, mimicking Faustinianus clutching his sheepskins. "'My ledgers! Twenty years of the portorium! The receipts!' Dropped his robes right in the mud and crawled after his inkpots while the roof fell on his head!"
Around the fire, the laughter rose into the granite cleft, loud and unburdened.
Tiata sat on a flat stone with her knees pulled up, resting her chin on her forearm. She had watched the whole display with a broad grin, her eyes bright in the firelight, laughing along with them at every fresh retelling. She didn't add her own barbs to the mockery—she didn't need to—but she enjoyed the spectacle of Rome tripping over its own boots as much as any of them.
She waited until the laughter began to taper off, until Tove was dishing out the hot peas into wooden bowls and the breathing around the hearth slowed.
"They'll be raking ash out of those two stations until midsummer," Tiata said, her voice warm, carrying the lingering humor of the moment.
She took the bowl Tove handed her, blew the steam from the surface, and let the quiet hold for a heartbeat before looking around the circle.
"We had our fun with Noricum," Tiata said, shifting the tone with easy, unforced purpose. "Now we have to get through to the east."
"What does the country look like past these bluffs?" Toria asked, looking across at Thilda.
Thilda shook her head, dipping her spoon into her bowl. "Don't ask me. My people held ground six hundred miles west of here, along the Neckar. Upper Germania I know. This granite is the furthest east I've ever set foot in my life."
Toria turned to Tiata. "You're from Dacia. Do you know this road?"
"Dacia is another world downriver," Tiata said quietly. "Between here and my mountains sits Pannonia. I know it belongs to Rome, and I know the Danube cuts right through the middle of it. Beyond that, we are riding into ground none of us have ever walked."
"They seem to be using the water as their border all through here," Thana observed, leaning back against her cedar root. "If there are big legions out there, their stone fortresses will probably sit on the riverbank to guard the shipping."
"I will ride out an hour before first light," Tigratavā said. "Take the mare up the eastern ridge and see where these rocks break. If the bluffs open into farmland, I'll find where the timber runs and where Rome has its watch posts before we take the horses down into the open."
Tiata nodded, satisfied. "That seems reasonable. If you spend the day tomorrow scouting along the river, the rest of us can do a bit of foraging nearby, and replenish our stocks. We might even meet a fully laden supply train."

12 — The Last Wagon
An hour before first light, the granite cleft was black and silent.
Tigratavā rose without disturbing the gravel. She checked her mare’s hooves in the dark, ran a hand down the animal's legs, and tightened the cinch. Her gorytos and recurve bow were already slung across her back.
Tiata shifted on her bedroll by the dead hearth, her eyes opening in the shadows. The two women traded a single nod. Tigratavā took the mare’s lead rope, led the horse down the loose scree on the balls of her feet, and melted into the eastern timber.
By dawn, grey light had filtered through the bare canopy of the Dunkelsteinerwald.
Tove was the first on her feet, blowing life into the buried embers and heating water in the bronze kettle. The others stirred, shaking the night’s frost from their cloaks, checking tack and feeding dry birch bark to the fire.
"Tigratavā will have reached the river flats by now," Thilda said, tightening the straps on her greaves.
"And we have empty grain bags," Toria said, tossing a dry leather nosebag aside. "The horses need oats, not scrub."
Thilda looked up through the bare branches at the wooded ridge above them. "The tracks coming down off the interior plateau hug the high contour. If anything is moving toward the Danube, it will come through the narrow pass."
They saddled their seven mounts and rode out as the sun cleared the ridges.
For two hours they worked east through dense stands of spruce and damp birch, keeping below the crests to avoid outlining themselves against the sky. The granite country was cut by deep runoff ravines where snowmelt roared toward the Danube, drowning the sound of their hooves.
It was Thilda who caught the first sign.
She pulled up on a forested shoulder and pointed down through the hazel scrub.
Two hundred paces below them, cut into the steep hillside, ran a secondary military track dropping down from the interior plateau toward the Danube. Deep ruts were gouged into the yellow mud, and drifting up through the trees came the squeal of iron-tired wheels and wooden brake blocks.
They tied their mounts in a brushy hollow out of sight and moved to the edge on foot.
Below them, a Roman freight convoy was descending the pass in single file. Four heavy freight wagons, their oak beds covered by arched hoops of greased cattle hide lashed tight to the sideboards. Each wagon was pulled by a pair of heavy draught oxen, their breath rolling from broad nostrils as they braced their hooves against the descent.
On the steep slope, the wagons kept fifteen paces between them. Brake blocks shrieked against the hubs, iron chains clattered against timber, and the carters shouted hoarse curses at their teams, their eyes locked on the outer ruts.
An auxiliary optio rode his mule twenty paces ahead of the lead wagon. Two foot soldiers walked beside the second team, while three auxiliary infantrymen trailed fifty paces behind the last wagon, their shields slung across their shoulders, their heads down against the wet wind.
"Army freight," Tove murmured, her eyes on the bulging hide tilts. "That's grain from the interior depots."
A hundred paces down the slope, the track cut hard around a granite shoulder, forming a blind hairpin bend. The cliff wall on the right blocked the view ahead, while the roar of the mountain creek below drowned any noise from the rear.
"That rock shoulder shuts out everything behind it," Taran observed, looking at the ancient oak leaning out over the road. "And there's a good sturdy bough hanging right over the track."
Without another word, the group divided and took their ground.
They left the seven horses tied to dwarf pines eighty paces up a side gully, out of sight and scent of the road, and slipped down the slope.
Tiata, Tove, and Thilda took their ground behind a granite outcrop just past the apex of the bend. Ten paces down the slope, hidden in the brush along the road verge, Thana, Toria, and Tacita crouched in the briars. Above the track, Taran worked her way onto the heavy oak limb, balancing on her haunches, her weight resting on the bark.
The rumble grew loud as the column approached.
The lead wagon reached the hairpin. The carter leaned his weight into the brake lever, hauling on his left rein, and the heavy wagon creaked around the rock. The second followed. Then the third.
The rock shoulder cut off the line of sight.
Below the crag, the iron wheels of the three lead wagons ground against the stone, their shrieking brakes echoing off the cliff face.
The fourth wagon approached the turn.
Its two draught oxen lumbered forwards, their yokes creaking, their heads down as they reached the apex of the bend.
Behind the wagon, thirty paces back, the three rear guards walked with their cloaks clutched against their throats, looking at the mud beneath their sandals.
The carter sat on his bench, bracing both feet against the timber footboard, his hands white on the leather lines.
Taran dropped from the bough, her weight coming down across his shoulders from behind. The impact drove him forward and down into the footwell, his face smashing into the floorboards before he could draw breath. She came down hard on his back, a knee between his shoulder blades pinning his weight, while her hand clamped over his mouth and her blade found his throat. The leather lines slipped from his lifeless fingers, trailing into the mud.
Simultaneously, Tiata and Tove broke from the shadow of the crag.
Tove brought her heavy axe down with a two-handed sweep, shattering the yoke-beam and driving the iron head into the skull of the near ox. Beside her, Tiata stepped inside the offside horn, bringing the falx around in a low, concussive arc that cleaved the beast’s forelegs clean through.
Both oxen collapsed into the mud, their heavy weight driving the wagon tongue into the earth and wedging the front wheels against the rock face.
The wagon stopped dead.
At the tailgate, the sudden halt made the lead rear guard look up, his hand reaching for his spear.
Before he could shout, Tacita stepped out from the brush beside the rock wall. Her gladius drove upward beneath his jaw, catching him under the ear and bearing his weight down into the slush without a sound.
Thilda stepped into the road directly behind the second man, driving her framea through his mail cuirass, pinning his chest against the rock.
The third guard spun round, reaching for his spatha, but Toria closed the distance in two strides. Her falcata swept across his throat before the blade cleared the scabbard. Thana lunged forwards, catching his falling shield by the iron rim before it struck the cobbles, easing the dead man into the ditch.
The entire strike took twelve seconds.
Around the rock shoulder, forty paces down the mountain, the remaining three wagons continued their noisy descent, their iron wheels grinding on the stone, their carters oblivious to the road behind them.
Thana drew her xiphidion and slashed the rawhide ropes binding the rear flap of the wagon tilt. She hauled the greasy cattle hide aside, exposing the cargo bed.
Sacks of grain were stacked three deep, flanked by barrels of salt pork and linen hampers of dried peas.
Tove threw her axe to the ground, caught the nearest burlap sack by the corners, and heaved it out onto the road. "Horse oats. Whole grain."
Toria and Thilda were already hauling sacks across the ditch into the side gully. Tove hoisted a second sack of oats onto her broad shoulder, while Thana cut away three heavy slabs of smoked pork hanging from the center hoop, rolling them into a strip of clean canvas.
Tacita found a small wooden keg wedged behind the meal bins, smashed the head with the butt of her gladius, and scooped two linen bags full of coarse rock salt.
They worked with quiet, practiced speed.
Within two minutes, four sacks of grain, three slabs of bacon, a sack of dried peas, and the salt were carried up to the waiting mounts.
The sound of the other three wagons was fading down the gully, moving toward the lower valley without checking pace.

13 — The Beneficiarius at Namare
The smell of wet soot and burned olive oil hung thick in the narrow granite pass.
Beneficiarius Marcus Ulpius Aper stepped through the charred doorway of the gatehouse, his iron-studded boots crunching on broken roof slates and soggy ash. Water from the bucket line dripped from the ceiling timbers onto his bronze shoulder-guards, hissing against the hot stone of the hearth.
In the corner, curled on the damp flagstones, sat the scribe, his forearms wrapped in grease-stained linen, staring blankly at the floor.
Aper kicked a blackened piece of pine shelving out of his path. "Get up and go to the barracks. Sit by the brazier and hold your tongue."
He turned back to the threshold and surveyed the defile.
The fire was out, but the post was in ruins. Smoke rose in thin grey wisps from the roof frame, drifting out across the swollen current of the Danube. Down on the road, two auxiliary guards were hauling the splintered remnants of the oak turnpike barrier into the ditch, their tunics soaked with muddy water. A third guard sat on the ditch bank holding a broken collarbone, while the wine carter stood ten paces off, wailing about his ruined casks.
Aper walked to the center of the road.
He was forty-four years old, a seasoned veteran detached from Legio II Italica to hold the governor’s road police station at Namare. He had governed the checkpoint for four years, settling disputes between bargees, checking transit passes, and logging imperial couriers.
He stopped beside the slotted timber receiver post where the barrier arm had rested. The wood of the receiver was intact, bearing no splinter marks or crushed grain from a forced blow. Aper crouched and scanned the mud at the base of the rock wall. Two paces into the shadow of the cliff, half-buried in yellow slush, lay the iron locking pin.
Aper picked it up. The pin had not been sheared by the impact of the wine cart, but had been worked loose by hand before the ox ever touched the wood.
He rose and turned the heavy iron pin in his palm.
"Decurion," Aper called out.
The watchtower commander, his face smeared with black soot, trotted across the road and came to attention.
"Bring the arrow from the carter's ox," Aper ordered. "And bring whatever the men raked from the records room hearth."
The decurion returned moments later, carrying a heavy iron broadhead sticky with dark blood, and laid a second object on an upturned cask. It was a heavy trilobate iron head forged for a composite recurve bow, burned black by the oil fire, its tang twisted from impact.
Aper held the two arrowheads side by side. They were completely different in weight, length, and origin, confirming two distinct archers.
"Who was on the tower platform when the roof caught?" Aper asked.
"Varro and Lucius, sir," the decurion said. "They were watching the dispute at the barrier. When the room went up, they ran for the water buckets."
"And the riders?"
"They came from the west, round the blind shoulder of the rock," the decurion said. "Four abreast filling the width of the road, with four more right behind them, cutting through the gap before the bucket line could drop their pails."
"Eight riders," Aper said. "Describe them."
The decurion shifted his weight in the mud, glancing toward the guards hauling timber in the ditch. "The guards who were knocked into the mud by the ox swear the front rider had a bronze cuirass and bare arms. They said the waist was narrow."
Aper looked at him with cold contempt. "You were overrun by eight mounted shock riders who coordinated an arson strike, breached an unpinned barrier with a stampeded ox, and cleared a defile in twenty heartbeats. Do not repeat tavern gossip on this road, Decurion. If an auxiliary mentions women again, dock his pay for a month and put him on ditch duty."
Before the decurion could answer, a dull, grinding screech echoed down the mountain pass.
Through the mist, an auxiliary optio on a shaggy mule came down the rocky grade, followed by the lead wagon of a heavy freight convoy. The draught oxen leaned back into their yokes, iron-shod wheels grinding against the stones as the carter hauled on his brake lever. Behind the first wagon came a second, its cattle-hide tilt dripping melting frost, with two foot auxiliaries walking alongside the team. Then came the third wagon, rolling to a halt behind the second while the driver wiped grit from his eyes.
Aper stood waiting, but the road behind the third wagon remained empty.
Aper looked at the optio. "Where is the rest of your column?"
The optio swung down from his mule, looking back over his shoulder with a frown. "We are four wagons out of Cetium, carrying grain, salt pork, and pulse for the river wharves."
"I count three," Aper said.
The optio turned around, squinting up the track. "He was thirty paces behind us when we took the upper grade, and the rear guards were right with him."
Aper turned on his heel toward the two mounted outriders waiting by the sentry hut. "Take the track and find that wagon."
The two cavalrymen spurred their horses up the rocky pass, their mounts' hooves clattering over the granite shelves before disappearing into the mist.
Aper did not stand idly while the riders were gone. He took the double-leaf wax manifest from the optio, cracked the seal, and read the tallies. The four wagons carried identical consignments of twenty burlap sacks of whole-grain horse oats, six timber casks of salt pork, ten linen hampers of dried peas, and three small kegs of coarse rock salt.
Aper walked to the lead wagon, had the driver throw back the cattle hide, and climbed onto the hub to inspect the stowage. He counted the sacks and casks, then moved to the second wagon and the third, repeating the inspection. Every wagon was packed to the exact same imperial pattern.
Twenty minutes passed before the drumming of hooves sounded from the upper slopes.
The two outriders burst out of the gully at a hard canter, their horses blowing hard as they hauled their reins.
"At the hairpin bend, a mile up," the lead trooper reported, his breath coming in ragged bursts. "The wagon is wedged hard against the crag. Both oxen are dead, the carter has had his throat cut, and the three rear guards are dead in the ditch."
Aper swung into the saddle of his black gelding. "Decurion, take four mounted troopers and follow me."
Ten minutes of hard trotting brought them up the pass to the blind hairpin bend, where the roar of the mountain creek drowned out every sound beyond five paces.
The fourth wagon sat wedged across the narrow shelf, its tongue driven into the mud. The near ox lay on its side with its skull caved in, while the offside beast had collapsed where it stood, its forelegs severed above the knees by a sweeping blade. The carter lay face down in the footwell with his throat opened to the bone, and the three rear guards lay dead in the ditch slush.
Aper dismounted to inspect the bodies.
The first guard had a clean puncture under his jaw where a blade had driven straight into the brain. The second was slumped against the granite wall, his mail shirt punched through by a heavy thrusting spear. The third lay sprawled on his back, his neck laid open from ear to ear, with his sword still locked inside its scabbard.
Aper walked to the rear of the wagon, pulled the slashed cattle-hide flap aside, and checked the cargo bed against the tallies he had verified below.
Four sacks of oats had been dragged from the top tier, leaving sixteen behind. Three slabs of salt pork had been sliced cleanly from the hanging hooks beneath the center hoop, while the casks remained untouched. One linen hamper of peas was gone from the forward corner, and one of the three small salt kegs had its head smashed in, with a small portion scooped out from the top.
Aper stepped back onto the road and examined the rest of the scene.
The three riveted mail cuirasses remained strapped to the dead guards, their bronze-fitted belts were intact, and their four military spathas lay in the slush where they had fallen. Under the carter's bench, the heavy oak tool chest sat bolted to the frame with its bronze padlock unbroken, the spare iron chains hung from their hooks, and the carter's leather coin purse remained tucked into his belt.
